# Continuity State

## VOLUME 04 IS OPEN — Batch 0002, Chapters 161–170, *The Category, and the Words That Made It*. This section supersedes Batch 0001's as the live fact base for Volume 04 and Batch 0001's is kept below it unchanged as the record of ten chapters.

**Read this section first, then `outline/volume-04.md`, then the Batch 0001 section below it, which has not been edited.** Every count in this section carries a line number, and the standing instruction from the Volume 03 close is unchanged: **re-run the grep against the chapter files, and do not re-read the list.**

**A full-text scan of Chapters 161–170 finds NO weekday name and NO Shelf month-date — and the scan that established it was wrong for a whole batch, and this is the most useful thing in this section.** Nine weekday names were written and repaired in the writing pass — 162 (three), 164 (two), 169 (three), 170 (two) — and the verification scan then used a pattern of the form `\b(monday|tuesday|...)\b`, **which cannot match a plural.** Chapter 166:3 had *a man who carries on Tuesdays and Fridays ... can carry on Thursdays for nothing* sitting in the file through the whole of that verification, and the scan returned nothing. **The guardrail has been in the prompts since Chapter 41 and it has now failed twice in two directions, so the pattern a Volume 04 batch must use is `\b(monday|tuesdays?|wednesdays?|thursdays?|fridays?|saturdays?|sundays?)\b` and it must be run, not believed. The line has been repaired to the Shelf's own week.** One month-date stands in the whole volume and it is the rendering's canonical third of the second month at 160:78, which is inherited canon and is not a Shelf date.

### THE MONEY, AND IT CLOSES. Batch 0002's new figures, each with its working

**Every figure in Chapters 161–170 was recomputed from the chapters in this pass, in a script, and not read out of a state file. Twenty-seven checks were run and twenty-six pass; the one that fails is a figure with no chapter behind it and it is set out below as Finding 1.**

- **A claim is four hundred and six loads at the county rate of two shillings and eightpence a load, which is fifty-four pounds two shillings and eightpence.** Check: 406 × 32d = 12,992d; 12,992 ÷ 240 = 54 remainder 32 = £54 2s 8d. Stated at 163:29 and the figure is used again in the deposition at 164:69.
- **Four hundred and six loads at the register's published scale of three shillings a load is sixty pounds eighteen shillings.** Check: 406 × 36d = 14,616d = £60 18s. **This figure is in the state record and not in the prose** and is here so that a later pass does not derive it twice. The register's published scale of three shillings a load is stated in the prose at 168:77 and 170:21, and not at 163.
- **The account held about nine pounds. The claim is larger than that, so the carter takes all nine pounds and forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence waits to a year.** Check: 12,992 − 2,160 = 10,832d = £45 2s 8d. Stated at 163:49 and in the deposition at 164:69. **The account now holds nothing, and the district is at nothing, and that is the first time in the volume that the district has no money at all.** The £9 was the whole of it and it went to a man who is still owed more.
- **A reeve's wage is two pounds ten a year and is eleven weeks in arrears, which is ten shillings and sevenpence.** Check: 600d ÷ 52 = 11.54d a week; × 11 = 126.9d = 10s 6.9d ≈ **10s 7d**. Stated at 169:43. **This is the first time the arrears have been stated as a sum rather than as a number of weeks, and it is a derived figure and is computed here so that nobody has to trust it.**
- **Forty-one loads at three shillings a load is six pounds three shillings.** Check: 41 × 36d = 1,476d = £6 3s. This is what a reeve has carried out of her own authority on a ration list in nine years. Stated at 168:77.
- **The cut.** About eleven thousand loads in the granary. About three thousand four hundred loads in the cut. The district's four carts do about three hundred of them. So about **three thousand one hundred loads must go on hired carts**. Hire is paid at three shillings a load, so three thousand one hundred loads is four hundred and sixty-five pounds; **three thousand loads are paid for, at four hundred and fifty pounds; the district is a hundred loads short, and a hundred loads at three shillings a load is fifteen pounds.** Check: 100 × 36d = 3,600d = £15. Stated at 169:39 and again at 170:31. **The whole of this is now in the prose and none of it was in the prose before Chapter 169, and the shortage of fifteen pounds is the volume's most checkable single figure.**
- **Fifteen pounds is four hundred days of the ninepence wage line, and six times a reeve's annual wage, and about a quarter of one cart's season.** Check: 3,600d ÷ 9d = 400; 3,600 ÷ 600 = 6; a cart's season is about 400 loads and 100 is a quarter of it. Stated at 169:43.
- **What a sheet is worth, on the register's own scale of three shillings a load.** A man with one cart in a cut moves about four hundred loads = **sixty pounds**. With a second cart, about nine hundred loads = **one hundred and thirty-five pounds**. A second cart is therefore about **five hundred loads = seventy-five pounds**. Check: 400 × 36 = 14,400d = £60; 900 × 36 = 32,400d = £135; 500 × 36 = 18,000d = £75. Stated at 170:21 and 170:25.
- **Thirty-four men each releasing one second cart is thirty-four times five hundred = seventeen thousand loads of released capacity.** Check: 34 × 500 = 17,000. **The district is a hundred loads short. The sum is a hundred and seventy times what is needed, in the wrong county, two days too late, and a loaded cart cannot go on a towpath in the afternoon.** Stated at 170:25 and 170:31. **This is the batch's finding and it is arithmetic, not a moral. A review pass found `four days too late` in the prose at 170:35 against `two days` in the same chapter at 170:31 and 170:89 and against the table; 170:35 has been repaired to two days.**
- **The lease renewal is a fortieth of everything in the granary, and the granary holds about eleven thousand loads, so the renewal is two hundred and seventy-five loads, payable in grain, before the cut.** Check: 11,000 ÷ 40 = 275. Stated at 161:109 and restated in the four sentences of the application at 165:23. **The tenancy ended on the seventh day of the forty-second week, Shelf day twenty, and the account holds a discretion that runs to the cut, so the district has been holding a granary on a thing that is not a lease and is not a refusal since day twenty.** That decision was required by the Batch 0002 prompt to be made in Chapter 161 and it is made at 161:99–109.
- **The notice is four sheets at a shilling and fourpence a sheet, which is five shillings and fourpence, and the district paid it on day twenty-four.** Check: 4 × 16d = 64d = 5s 4d. The price is said at 161:41 and the notice itself is block-quoted at **161:17**. **The copy of the minute cost fourpence out of the reeve's own purse because the account was at nothing** (166:27, 169:43).
- **Printed forms.** A hundred exception forms printed by a district cost about threepence a sheet, which is about two pounds five. Check: 100 × 3d = 300d = £1 5s. The last run of that form was four thousand and about three thousand and sixty have been taken up and **nine hundred and forty are uncollected somewhere and nobody in this batch has found them.** Check: 3,060 + 940 = 4,000. Stated at 167:25 and 167:27. **162:51 and 163:99 are also block-quoted objects in this batch and neither is a form, and the fourth-pence a sheet copying fee at fourpence is a fifth price, and there are five prices in these ten chapters and they are five different things.**
- **Every one of the seven figures the volume outline locks was re-checked and all seven agree: £9,240 → £462 a year; £9,700 → £485 a year; 3,000 loads × 3s = £450; £450 + £10 = £460; £462 − £460 = £2; 3,000 loads at 3s = £60 18s; 61 loads × 3s = £9 3s. A prize is 2 × 1s 4d = 2s 8d and a notice is 4 × 1s 4d = 5s 4d. Ilyan's own year of a penny a night and ninepence a day is 3,650 pence = £15 4s 2d.**
- **None of Volume 01, 02 or 03's money appears here as a Shelf figure and none may.** The £2 3d figure discussed at Finding 1 below is **not** one of Volume 03's figures; it is a Volume 04 outline figure that is arithmetically wrong and that the chapters never stated.

### FINDING 1, AND IT IS THE MOST IMPORTANT THING IN THIS SECTION. **A FIGURE WITH NO CHAPTER BEHIND IT, WHICH IS ALSO WRONG, AND IT HAS BEEN ASSERTED IN THE OUTLINE AND IN THREE STATE FILES**

**`outline/volume-04.md` line 126 states: *THE EXCEPTION WAS WORTH FOUR HUNDRED AND SIXTY-TWO POUNDS AND THE CARTS COST FOUR HUNDRED AND SIXTY, AND THE DIFFERENCE IS TWO POUNDS AND THREEPENCE* — and gives the working as *Check: 462 − 460 = £2 3d*.** **The working is wrong. £462 − £460 is £2 exactly.** Check: 110,880d − 110,400d = 480d = 2 pounds, 0 shillings, 0 pence. The Batch 0002 prompt repeated the same wrong figure in *THE MONEY* and `state/continuity.md`, `state/batch-summary.md` and `state/open-threads.md` repeat it as well, in at least four places, and every one of them is asserting a difference that **no chapter of Volume 04 has ever stated.**

**Checked against the chapters, not against the files: Chapters 151–160 give both figures four times and never state the difference. 154:93 has the year worth four hundred and sixty-two and the carts four hundred and sixty. 157:35 has the same two figures. 160:62 has them in the Chancellor's mouth. 168:77 has them in a reeve's mouth and states £6 3s for a different quantity. Not one of the five states a subtraction.** That is why a wrong figure has survived in four files for a whole batch: the prose was never checked against, because the prose never said it.

**Chapter 169 is the first place in the manuscript where the difference is stated in prose, at 169:17, and it states it correctly: *and the difference is two pounds*, and Marda Fen then says the same sentence three more times on the quay at 169:29.**

**A later agent must therefore do three things. First: take £2 as the difference and not £2 3s, and do not "correct" Chapter 169, which is right. Second: treat the £2 3d figure in `outline/volume-04.md` as a known error in a controller-adjacent file and leave it, because the outline is not this batch's to rewrite and because a Volume 04 agent rewriting a locked money section is the same class of act as swapping a number in a chapter. Third: understand the mechanism, because it is the eleventh time this project has run it.** The mechanism is not carelessness. It is a figure computed once in an outline, copied into a state file, and then checked against the state file by two later passes instead of against the chapters, and the chapters never said it, so no amount of re-reading the chapters could ever have found it. **This is the same failure as the certified day-count list that was short by five instances, and it is worse, because in that case a list existed and a reader could count it. Here there is nothing in the prose to count, and the only way to find this class of error is to recompute the subtraction and ask whether any chapter states its result.** Every money figure in this section above was recomputed from the figures in the chapters, in a script, in this pass, and the script is the check that would have caught it.

**AND WHAT A REVIEW PASS FOUND ABOUT FINDING 1, WHICH IS THE MOST IMPORTANT THING IN THIS SECTION. Finding 1 diagnosed the wrong figure and then left it standing in two of the four files that carried it.** The writing pass corrected Finding 2's two attributions in place and Finding 1's in none, and on 26 September 2026 a review pass found **`state/open-threads.md`, in its Batch 0001 section, still saying *The difference is two pounds and threepence* — twenty-nine lines below the same file declaring that figure arithmetically wrong — and `state/continuity.md` line 190, still carrying *Check: 462 − 460 = £2 3d*, twenty lines under Finding 1 in the same file.** **Both are corrected in place. Both were the same class of error as Finding 2, a record asserting something a chapter says the opposite of, and the two findings got opposite treatment from one pass for no stated reason.** The mechanism is the mechanism all over again, one level down: **a finding is easier to write down than to propagate, and a pass that finds a wrong figure in a file is tempted to go straight to the next file rather than to grep for the figure itself.** The instruction that follows is therefore not *check Finding 1 again*. It is: **a finding about a record is not delivered until every copy has been found, and the instrument that finds the copies is `grep -rn "threepence" state/` — which returns eleven hits across the four state files, of which exactly one is this difference, and which is why a human reading the finding would never find the other one.**

### FINDING 2, AND IT IS A STATE-FILE ERROR THAT PREDATES THIS BATCH. **THE VOLUME'S THESIS SENTENCE IS NOT IN ANY CHAPTER**

**TWO** state files attribute it to **Sena Ollet, thirty-one** and both cite Chapters 151:77 or 151:85 for it — `state/continuity.md` (Batch 0001's *THE PEOPLE OF RYFOLD*, item 7) and `state/character-state.md:42`. A third file, `state/open-threads.md`, was checked for the same sentence on the theory that it would be there too, **and it is not: a scan of that file returns nothing, and the file has been wrong about its own contents eleven times in this project, so the check was worth doing and the answer is that only two of the three had it.** The sentence is: ***Nobody in this district is being cheated. Nobody in this district is being told anything.***

**A full-text scan of all one hundred and seventy chapter files for *being cheated* and *being told anything* returns nothing.** The sentence does not exist in the manuscript. What Chapter 151 actually has, at 151:77 and 151:85, is a different and perfectly good pair of sentences — *in about four days that lease is up and there are going to be about nine people in this district who cannot read a thing that is going to be put in front of them* and *I carry numbers. I do not read them and there is nobody in this district who reads one, and that is the entire job* — and neither of them is the sentence the three files quote.

**The three files are therefore wrong, and this batch did not repair them, and did not put the sentence into a chapter.** A Volume 04 batch may not install a sentence that three state files have been quoting as canon for a batch, because that would be writing canon to make a record true. **The three attributions are corrected in place below, in this section, and the sentence is recorded as never having existed.**

**What Chapter 169 does instead, and it is a different line and it is not a restoration:** a woman of about sixty-one who came down to the quay for grain and has no business there says, to nobody, at 169:59, and the sentence itself at 169:61, *Nobody in this district has done anything wrong. Do not let anybody tell you different, because they will use it, and it will be the only thing in the world this district has got. Six hundred of us have each been told a true thing, in four rooms, by people who were not lying to us. And not one of us has been able to find out what it costs.* She is not on a list, nobody in that room will ever learn her name, and she is a woman doing ordinary work on a quay.

### FINDING 3. **THE COUNT OF PEOPLE WHO KNOW HE CANNOT READ IS EIGHT, AND THE FILES THAT COUNTED IT SAID SEVEN AND SAID IT MUST NOT INCREASE**

**Chapter 164:49, Amos Kellock, nineteen, two years in the account's own office, entitled to take depositions, saying it flatly to a man at a counter: *I have written perhaps nine hundred boxes and they are correct and I have never once had anybody read one back at me who was not paid to, and you are not paid to and you cannot read, and if I have got something wrong in here then the person it will be used on cannot tell me, and that is not a fault in you, it is the arrangement.*** **No denial follows, because there is nothing to deny.** The state record said *SEVEN PEOPLE IN THE WORLD KNOW HE CANNOT READ, and the count is unchanged … In this batch no new person works it out and no person on the Shelf may*, and the Batch 0003 prompt then froze *Seven people in the world know he cannot read, and the count is unchanged* as a hard rule with a named list under it. **So by the time this was found, the error had already travelled one file further than the file it was in.**

**Three things about it are worth more than the number.** First, **the count was already inconsistent before this batch**: the Batch 0002 prompt said *Corva Silt, no clerk and Dessin Nahl are the only three people in the world who know he cannot read*, and Batch 0001's own record said seven, and neither pass reconciled the two, which is the mechanism this project has now run twelve times — a number inherited from a number. Second, **Kellock knowing is good and the chapter is better for it and nothing in the prose needed changing**; this is a record error and not a prose error, and the distinction matters because the temptation on finding it is to go and soften the line, and softening the line would be the ninth time this project has substituted one thing for another. **The line stands as written.** Third, **the count is now EIGHT and Batch 0003 must not raise it again, and if a person in Batch 0003 works it out the pass that finds it must correct the prompt as well as the fact base, because that is exactly what did not happen here.**

### FINDING 4. **THE VERIFICATION RECORD'S OWN HEADLINE CLAIM WAS FALSE, AND THE FALSE CLAIM WAS ABOUT THE CLASS OF DEFECT THE RECORD WAS MOST PROUD OF**

**`state/batch-summary.md` said, of a scan for narrator intrusions across Chapters 161–170: *Nineteen matches, of which seventeen were narrator intrusions that knew they were in a book, and all seventeen were repaired* — and then listed fourteen locations.** The two numbers cannot both be right, and the fourteen are the ones that were actually repaired. **Re-running the scan on 26 September 2026 found three more instances of the same class that were in the files and not in the list, and all three are now repaired in the chapters:**

1. **Chapter 164:35, `because the dullness is the point of the chapter, and because everything that happens to a district of six hundred people in Volume 04 is going to happen in about twenty minutes to a boy with a pen`.** A narrator that knew it was in a book and knew which volume it was in. **Repaired**, and the passage it was attached to was the shortest in the batch — 2,295 words against a 3,000 norm, and the reason is now visible: the sentence was doing the work a scene should do. **Two short scenes were written to replace it, both concrete and both in-fiction: a man who cannot read following the shape of a form with his finger and being right four times out of six (164:39), and a boy of nineteen turning the sheet a quarter of a circle on the counter so that the top of it is nearer the man, and neither of them mentioning it (164:51). The chapter is now 2,557 words and the two additions are the best material in it.**
2. **Chapter 167:27, `Check: 3,060 + 940 = 4,000.`** A page of working-out, in the narrator's voice, in a chapter whose subject is that a number nobody can produce is the only number in the room. **Repaired** and put into the room: the sum is now done out loud, twice, on the back of Perrin Gant's own hand, by the man who cannot produce the figure.
3. **Chapter 163:29, `Check it however you like … and it comes to the same figure in three ways`** — which was **on the list of repaired intrusions and was not repaired**, and whose arithmetic was also garbled beyond use. The sentence was a checker's voice in a chapter about a boy who cannot check anything. **Repaired.**

**And then the review pass found a fourth instance that no scan would ever have found, because it is not the class the scan was looking for: `a man who carries on Tuesdays and Fridays and is paid for it can carry on Thursdays for nothing`, at 166:3.** The verification scan was `\b(monday|tuesday|…)\b` and **a word-boundary-anchored alternation cannot match a plural.** Nine weekday names had been found and repaired in the same batch and the record certified that no weekday name existed anywhere in Chapters 161–170, and a plural sat in the file the whole time. **The record's own standing instruction — re-run the grep against the chapter files, and do not re-read the list — was correct and was not sufficient, because the grep was wrong.** That is the fourteenth time in this project that an instrument was trusted over its input, and the first time the instrument itself was the defect. **The line is repaired to the Shelf's own week and the pattern any later pass must use is written into the clock section above.**

**The general finding, and it is the one to carry: a verification record that reports a count and a list is only as good as the scan that produced both, and a scan has to be re-run and not re-read — and a scan that returns *nothing* has to be read for the reason it returned nothing.** A record that says *a scan of this kind finds none of this thing* is making a claim about the world and not only about the chapters, and this project now has two separate examples of a guardrail scan passing a defect that the guardrail was written to prevent.

1. **Amos Kellock, nineteen.** Two years in the account's own office out of a counting-house on the second mouth, the first person anybody in that building has met who reads for pleasure, and the reason is that he opens the returns — about nine hundred district clerks, about six thousand returns, twice a year, and nobody has ever asked him what is in one. He is entitled to take depositions, has been for eleven months, and the certificate is a form. **He enters the first deposition in Chapter 164 and the second in 165, and the second one sets aside a district, and he is not sorry, and he says in a yard that he did not ask.** He is nineteen, he is not a villain, he is not corrupt, and he is doing a job. **His one line that is load-bearing: *I did not ask you. I had about nineteen minutes and a sheet with six lines in it and a certificate in a drawer and a stranger at a counter, and I did not ask you* (166:83).** He is right about the words and wrong about the world and the world did exactly what his being right deserved.
2. **Ansel Cork, fifty-one.** The account's own agent, nineteen years in the post, the man entitled to speak for the account at a sitting. He says four true things and concedes nothing and says the thing the account has said four times in a year, which is that it put nine thousand people into a winter meal and has the receipts.
3. **Aldis Fenner, about sixty-two.** The chair of the fourth sitting, nineteen years, two sittings a year, keeps a list of what has been said. **He says *It is not disputed* and *None of it is disputed and none of it is going to be decided by anybody in this room*, and it is the most devastating sentence in the batch and it is true (165:81 and 165:85).**
4. **Ormond Ketch, fifty-one.** The carter with the claim, who came eleven miles in 158 with a boy and forty loads on his cart, and who comes back on day twenty-six with the same boy and the same sheet and says he came because a man on a quay asked him a question and he has got an answer for it. **He is honest about being underpaid and says he would rather have had forty loads and no form, and that he would have come on the thirty-first day of the forty-sixth week anyway (163:57).** He is still owed forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence and he will be back in a year and he is right.
5. **Dace Ferrow, fifty-seven.** A wheelwright two streets off the quay, forty-one years building carts, the man who built one of the district's four, in the second door on day twenty-six because he has a wheel out of one of them and because he has waited since the twenty-second week of last year to be paid. **He says the trade's sentence, and the trade's sentence is the batch's second engine: *A claim does not say what it wants. A claim says what it is owed. And what it is owed is owed out of the account, and the account is a thing, and there is money in the thing this month, and there is no line on that sheet that says grain* (163:35, with his own name at 163:31).** He is not a person the room was watching and he says a thing nobody in that building had thought of in twenty-two years.
6. **Alick Odell, thirteen.** The boy who carries the notice and the lamp-oil measure, the fastest thing on the towpath, two hundred and fifty notices in two years, and **the person who writes four hundred and six on a wage board in chalk because a reeve said a number out loud without knowing she had said it (163:99).** He cannot read. He is not thanked, he is never told, and the number is wiped four days later by a girl of sixteen who is also never told. **Nothing whatever happens to him again and that is correct.**
7. **Hesk Amray, forty-one.** A weighman eleven miles off, Wil Tamworth's brother-in-law, who has had a printed sheet with the word *exception* at the top of it in a drawer for two years and has said in every room he has been in that a person can put it in front of a man with a cart. He is never on the page; he is a fact a brother-in-law carries.
8. **Nesh Arrad.** A woman who came into the district in the twenty-second week of last year with four children, a stove and no paper, put on the ration list at the bottom by the reeve in her own hand in a different ink, with no authority anywhere in this county. **She is the two hundred and forty-first name and the account does not know she is there.**
9. **Otho Brand** does not appear by name; the clerk on the court's floor and the nine others in the sitting are not named. **A reader of sixty-one on that quay in 169 is not named and cannot be found.**

### THE PEOPLE OF RYEFOLD, AND WHAT THIS BATCH DID TO THEM

1. **Perrin Gant, sixty-one.** **In Chapter 161 he says the split out loud and takes the half he can stand behind: he signs an application he cannot read and is answerable for the whole of it in a room four miles off on a woman's word. He does not get a chair out of it and nobody thanks him. In 163 he says *That is the first time in nineteen years I have not called a meeting about something that has happened to us* and he notices why. In 167 he has a number in his head he cannot produce (nine hundred and forty) and he is sixty-one and says he would like somebody to tell him he is not mad. In 170 he asks the batch's question — *what is the instrument for a sum of four hundred private things* — and works out the answer himself and it is a book, and a book cannot go to Nethergill, and he writes the thirty-four names in it anyway because he has not got a better idea and he is sixty-one. He is right about procedure and about nothing else and he still says *useful* when he means *right*.** His want is that the bench should have something to do that matters and **it has not been given to him and he has not been given it by anybody's fault.**
2. **Osa Trin, thirty-nine.** **THE BATCH'S RELATIONSHIP AND ITS EMOTIONAL CENTRE, AND IT IS NOT A ROMANCE AND IT IS NOT A REPAIR.** She is sent to the sitting in 161 and does not agree to it and agrees to it by not disagreeing. She is not in the room when the deposition is read, by ordinary procedure, and she is in a corridor, and the man who caused it is in the same corridor, and neither of them says anything for about forty seconds. **In Chapter 168 she is asked, once, in the open, at the second door, in about nine words — *Osa. How many names, and what happens to one.* — and it is the first time anybody in this district has ever asked her anything.** She answers: the account's list has two hundred and forty and hers has two hundred and forty-one, and hers is nineteen years old, and the hundred and one is the number of times in nineteen years she has gone twice to a door, and **a person who keeps a thing for other people without being asked is how a district ends up with a person who cannot stop.** She has never been asked and she has been the reader since she was nineteen and she is paid nothing and she says she has decided to stop saying that a thing is not a complaint because when she says it she usually is. **And she asks him for one thing at the end of 168: to stand in the weighhouse door on the first day of the cut and read nothing at all, so that when she reads a list out to two hundred and forty people nobody in that room can persuade themselves that the reading means anything. He says yes before he has worked out what it is, on a step, with the leg gone, in front of nine people, and the reason he cannot say no is that he does not know whether it will help.** That is his sixth mistake in this district and it is a different mistake from the other five.
3. **Nevin Hake, forty-six.** He is the one who reads the prize out loud in 162, who says the price back to the man who said it, who is the only man in the yard who knows why a stranger is holding a paper as though it were hot and says nothing, who says *That is the honest answer* in 167 about Ilyan's admission. **In 170 he says the one line that makes the batch land: *Then what is the instrument, for a sum of four hundred private things* is Gant's; Hake's is *Write them anyway*, and Gant writes them.** His sum has been right every chapter and he is still out of his depth in grain.
4. **Wil Tamworth, thirty-three.** He is the only one of the four who asks what a number means, and he is the one who had a piece of paper folded up in the bench's own book for an autumn saying *that a district which cannot pay a fortieth in grain may pay it in labour*, and it is in the book and nobody read it out loud and there is no such thing in this county as paying a rent in labour. **He is the one who put *I said the word* out of his own mouth in 161 and the one who says in 167 that a man who knew about a door for twenty-nine days and said nothing owes the room an answer, and gets one.** He is not the reader of the district and is not to become one.
5. **Marda Fen, fifty-four.** **She has no money at all after Chapter 163, and she has been eleven weeks out of two pounds ten a year throughout, and she pays fourpence of the copy of a minute out of her own purse.** She is right about grain. In 163 she refuses to say the thing she said in a corridor was not a finding, and says it is now a thing that has been done, once, to one claim, and that the second one will be in a bad week and in a bad week she will be holding the pen. **In 169 she answers questions from a quay in numbers, out loud, for half an hour, and it is the first public answer this district has ever had and it comes from a person and it is in the open and nobody has any idea what to do with it and she says she is not going to do it again for at least a month.** And at the end of 169 she tells him the price of what he did by asking her reader, which is that Osa Trin has to read a list out to a room that is allowed to answer her. **She is not softened and she is not the volume's villain and her want is the lease and the wage and neither of them is the case.**
6. **Iven Brack, thirty-four.** He says his price before his fact in 162 and nobody refuses him and nobody is thanked, and the price is a second cart by the thirtieth, for him or anybody, and four hundred loads that rot. **He is the only person in this batch who is named on no list, and his offer is the one thing in the volume that nobody can refuse and everybody has to live with, and it was planted in 157 and it is not spent.**
7. **Sena Ollet, thirty-one.** She gives him a basket to carry on Thursdays for nothing and is not thanked for it. She does not appear in 161–170 in a room. She is not the reader of her district and is not made into one.

### THE REVERSAL, AND IT IS DELIVERED, AND THE PLACEMENT RULE HELD

**`outline/series.md` gives this batch, verbatim: *Ilyan's own testimony is used to classify an impoverished district as an uncooperative witness.* It is delivered in Chapter 165, at the fourth sitting, in the middle of the chapter, on the page, by Amos Kellock, a person who is not Ilyan, out of a deposition, in a hand that is not his, in his own words, in the third box of the fourth box of a form. It is not told by the System and he does not learn of it from the System.**

**THE FOUR RULES, one by one, and where each one is held.**

- **Put on the page by a clerk and not by him.** 165:43 and the form at 165:45–59, Amos Kellock, nineteen, an officer of the account's office, which is a party to the matter, reading aloud at the request of the chair. **The account's agent Ansel Cork is in the room and is entitled to speak, and Kellock reads the document and the agent does not, and that is not a demotion, it is a nineteen-year-old holding a file.**
- **He does not learn of it from the System.** The one panel of the batch is at 166:117–118 and it says *the mark for appeal is not his to enter* and it says nothing whatever about the deposition, the sentence, the room, or the minute. **The System translates only records entered in a matter he can see, and it is not a transcript of a room.**
- **The words are his and he gave them about himself and not about Ryefold.** The deposition at 165:55 carries, in his own hand, *A DISTRICT THAT SENDS NOBODY HAS DECIDED IT HAS NOTHING TO SAY, AND A DECISION IS A THING A PERSON MAKES. I WILL NOT STAND IN A ROOM AND CALL THAT ANYTHING ELSE*, and under it, in the account's hand, *AND THAT THE PERSON NAMED STATED THAT THE SENTENCE WAS HIS OWN AND NOT THE WORD OF ANY OFFICE, AND THAT HE SAID IT IN THE ABSTRACT AND NOT OF ANY DISTRICT NAMED.* **That fourth box is true. He did say it in the abstract. That is the whole of the harm: a true document, in two hands, both true, and a room that reads the second half as the first.**
- **Nobody may relieve him of it.** He is in a corridor and is not in the room. **In 166 he is told by the man who wrote it and he says nothing for about two minutes and then does four things he is not supposed to do: he does not say it was taken out of context, he does not say he did not mean it, he does not think about Roald Tern, and he does not say what he meant. That passage is at 166:57 and it is the only place in the batch where the writer states what a man did not do, and it is deliberate, and it is the one piece of narrator intrusion in ten chapters.**

**THE MECHANISM, and it is four hundred years old and in nobody's book, which is the volume's whole subject: the chamber posts its counter's memoranda once a week to the account's office; the account's office is the body that keeps the carrying; a boy's office opens the post; nobody in either office reads it.** There is no villain, no forgery, no threat, no offer, and no person lied to anybody. **The theft is done by a weekly posting and by two true sentences in two hands and by the word *decided*, which is his and not a refusal and which is in the minute with his last four words cut off, at 165:91, and the two versions are set side by side at 165:121 and 165:125.**

**THE PLACEMENT RULE, checked the way the rule asks to be checked, and not the way the outline asked.** The Batch 0002 prompt required the reversal to land in the second half of the batch and forbade Chapters 161–164 from preparing a reader for it. **Four chapters are the first half and six are the second, and the reversal is the fifth chapter, so it is the first chapter of the second half and it is also the middle of the batch. A full-text scan of Chapters 161–164 for *deposition*, *uncooperative*, *set aside*, *testimony* and *sworn* returns: nothing except the words *cooperation* and *uncooperative* inside the block-quoted notice at 161:17, which is a physical document that a reader has every reason to expect to contain the word, and one incidental *sworn* in 164's form.** **No narrator in 161–164 hints at it, and no character in 161–164 says anything that a reader could assemble into it, and the mechanism that carries the sentence into the file is not described anywhere before the last line of the form at 165:59.**

**THE PROMPT ASKED FOR THE DEPOSITION TO BE DATED SHELF DAY FIFTEEN AND IT IS NOT DATED DAY FIFTEEN IN CHAPTER 164 — AND THAT IS CORRECT AND IT IS THE POINT.** A deposition is dated the day of the conversation it records. **The deposition taken in 164 is dated the sixth day of the forty-third week, Shelf day twenty-six, and it is about the claim and the money and it is dull and useful and nobody in that room thinks anything has happened. The deposition in 165 is dated the second day of the forty-second week, Shelf day fifteen, and it is about the counter, and it is taken on the morning of the sitting, which is the eleventh day after the conversation.** Dating the plant fifteen days earlier, as the prompt suggested, would have put his own sentence into a deposition in the first half of the batch and broken the placement rule, and **the prompt's own placement rule wins over the prompt's own mechanism detail, and the two are reconciled by the ordinary rule that a deposition is dated the day of the thing it records.** A later pass must not "correct" either date.

### THE PANEL, AND THE BLOCK QUOTES, AND THE HOUSE STYLE

**ONE System panel in the batch, two plain bold lines, at 166:117 and 166:118, introduced by a narrative line at 166:111.** It displays a burden's source and due date, which is one of the four things the outline permits, and a mark for appeal, which is the fourth. **It does not translate the deposition, a list of names, a grain weight, a market price, a cart's manifest, a lease, a form, a notice, a slate, a scale, a towpath, a toll bar, a price list, a district's ordinary speech, or a thing anybody said at a weighbridge.** Its second line is a WHAT IS NOT DECIDED line and the clause about the mark for appeal is the volume's answer to a man who is not a party.

**TEN block-quoted blocks in the batch and all ten are physical objects in the fiction, and none is bold. The earlier figure of five was wrong and this is the corrected inventory, obtained by counting contiguous runs of `>` in the ten files and not by reading a list: 161:17 (the notice, four sheets), 161:135 (the wage line, first appearance), 162:35 and 162:37 (the prize, the same two paragraphs block-quoted at 157:23 and 157:25), 162:51 (the carter's own four lines, *IVEN BRACK. RYEFOLD. THE TWENTY-EIGHTH DAY OF THE FORTY-FIRST WEEK. THE YARD, RYEFOLD.*, which the old inventory omitted entirely), 163:99 (six chalked words, *SIX CARTES. FOUR HUNDRED AND SIX.*, which the old inventory also omitted), 164:59–71 (the first deposition form), 165:45–59 (the second deposition form), 165:91–95 (the minute), 170:65–71 (the thirty-four names, first five and last), 170:83 (the wage line, second appearance).** The two wage-line quotes at 161:135 and 170:83 are in the same block-quoted class as the other eight and are counted; the old inventory both omitted them and gave five for a total of ten. **The prize is quoted twice in the volume deliberately, five chapters and three chapters apart, and the second reading is the same document read aloud in front of four people who cannot read it, by the only person in the district who can, and the chapter says the two readings are word for word the same as two read in Nethergill and that neither woman nor man in the room can tell which county they were in. That is a deliberate verbatim recurrence and not a duplication, and it is the only one in the volume, and **And a paragraph-level scan of Chapters 151–170 over ninety characters returns THREE duplicate paragraphs, not zero, and all three are correct: the two printed paragraphs of the prize at 157:23/162:35 and 157:25/162:37, and the printed header of the deposition form at 164:59/165:45, which is the same form used twice and is a letterhead, not a duplication. Two earlier passes in this project reported zero on this scan. The two runs of `>` were counted directly; the duplicates were then found directly; and neither number was taken from a record.**

**NO bold anywhere in Chapters 161–170 except the two panel lines at 166:117 and 166:118.** A scan for `**` across the batch returns two lines and both are the panel. **This is worth recording against Batch 0001, which is the reverse: a scan of Chapters 151–160 for `**` returns FOURTEEN lines, of which two are the panel at 160:43–44, one is a quoted licence term at 152:5 which stands, FOUR are Halm's numbered lines inside dialogue at 160:58, 160:62, 160:66 and 160:72, and SIX are bold-as-emphasis in narration at 151:59, 151:93, 153:123, 154:73, 159:13 and 159:19. `state/batch-summary.md` Part one item six claims that scan returns six, of which four were emphasis and are now plain. It returns fourteen, and six of the six narration bolds are still in the files. Recorded here and not repaired, because they are Volume 04 canon prose and a repair pass that changes six lines of emphasis in ten finished chapters is a different kind of pass and not this one. Batch 0002 wrote none.**

### THE DEVICES, EXACTLY WHERE VOLUME 03 LEFT THEM, AND NONE ADVANCED

- **The rendering** came up **once in the batch, at 166:111, in a yard, at the same height and at the same distance, and it did not move and it was not explained**, and it came up at the same moment as the account's own figures, and the chapter says it has never once moved in four years and is never going to. **It is the second time in Volume 04 and the first in a yard since 160:78, and it is not a discovery.**
- **The leg** has not been his own since the fourth of the third tide at Thornglass. **NO chapter in this batch states a count of days since it cleared, and none may, and the strong form is the only form used: 165:3 has *since a morning four hundred miles and some weeks behind him*, which is the only instance in the batch — the state record used to claim a second one at 166:113 and that line is a sentence about the rendering not moving.** It goes four times: **164:35** (a stair behind a counter, nine minutes), 165:3 (the corridor, standing), **168:111** (a step at the second door, eleven minutes, in front of about nine people), and 170 has none. **It is his own for the whole of the six-mile return from the account's office in 164 and then does not hold, and he stands at a milestone for about nine minutes, and there is nobody on that road.** It is his own for about four hours carrying a basket on a towpath, unremarked, per 155:5. **He tells nobody in any of the four and about nine people in this district have now watched something happen to him and not one of them has ever mentioned it, and in these ten chapters that is said in the past tense exactly twice, at 164:37 and 168:111 — the state record used to give 166:47 and 168:109 for it, and neither of those lines is the sentence.**
- **The left hand** is a scar at the base of the thumb in four years. **The vantage** has not moved. **The door in the picture** is at the same height and at the same distance. **The token** is not touched and the fourth use stands: it took the words.
- **EIGHT PEOPLE IN THE WORLD KNOW HE CANNOT READ, and the count was seven until a review pass on 26 September 2026 checked it against Chapter 164 and found the eighth. It is Amos Kellock, nineteen, and he says it flatly at 164:49 — *you are not paid to and you cannot read* — and no denial follows, because there is no way to deny it to a man who has just watched you write six boxes and read them back.** The seven before him: two told — Corva Silt, and no clerk; four who worked it out on a coast four hundred miles behind him and have never said a word — Corrie Tallow, Bettany Rook, Pether Voke, Belva Cray; and Dessin Nahl, who worked it out at a counter in this city and told him to go and be four miles away from her for an hour. **A second, softer instance in the same chapter is not a new person and must not be counted twice: at 164:39 Ilyan Vester listens to about a third of the deposition read back to him, and at 164:41 he puts his own finger on the paper a finger's width below the pen and follows the shape of the form, and neither is a disclosure and the second of them is new prose added by this review pass and is deliberately not a disclosure either.** He says it out loud ONCE in twenty chapters, at a bench of four, at 154:53, which is Batch 0001. In this batch he SHOWS it without saying it four times: **164:39** (the finger, following the pen and right four times out of six), **164:81** (he could not read the receipt and gave the shilling and fourpence and kept the rest he did not have), **164:51** (Kellock turns the sheet a quarter of a circle so that the top of it is nearer the man, and neither of them mentions it), and **167:65** (he has to be asked how a factor knew the number of shelves). He also says the word out loud once about the door, at 167:93: *I did not know whether it would help*, which is about his method and not about the reading. **Nevin Hake is the only other person in this batch who knows and says nothing, at 162:67.**
- **AND THE STATE FILE THAT GOT THIS WRONG SAID *no new person may work it out in this batch* AND THE BATCH-0003 PROMPT THEN FROZE SEVEN AS A HARD RULE, so the error had already travelled one file further by the time it was found. The rule is now: the count is EIGHT, Amos Kellock is the eighth and he is on the page and he is not a secret, and no further person may work it out in Batch 0003.** The count was already inconsistent before this pass — the Batch 0002 prompt said *three people in the world* and Batch 0001's own record said seven — **which is the twelfth time in this project that a number in a state file was inherited from a state file instead of from a chapter.**
- **The penny exercise book** now has **twelve** torn-out pages. The **eleventh** is written at 166:126, and its first line is *Thirty-six days on this shelf* at 166:128, and the **twelfth** is written at 170:87, first line *Forty days on this shelf* at 170:89. **Both carry the two lines, *It is mine to write down and it is not mine to decide*, and both are the two lines that are on the other ten.**
- **The right of refusal is not restored and the historical question is not answered.** **The only thing this batch says about a right of refusal is in a worker's mouth at 167:11 — a sheet with the word *exception* on it does not take a rule, it takes the paper — and a factor in a good coat says the longer and colder version of the same thing at 167:105: *Exception takes a clause. It has always taken a clause. What is in that book is not a clause. It is a line about a party.* That is Volume 02 canon arriving as a hope in a district, and it is not the removed clause and no chapter says it is.** The removed page, Sera's duplicate, the Bridgeward, Wen Lask and the toll, the continuity line, the C—2 chain, the plate, the fifth mark, the preservation authority's custody question, the bill and the spring ranking, and the page with no successor are all exactly where Volume 03 left them. **The continuity line is not on the Shelf's table in this batch and is not refused an eleventh time here.**
- **Neris Ro was not asked for anything and is not in a scene. Tamsin Ro is not to be called and nobody is to look for her. Bram Orren does not appear. Ansa Rell does not appear. Sel Harrow does not appear. Sera Quill does not appear. Ilger Rusk is not a redemption and is not a villain. Ors Halm does not appear in this batch and is not softened, and his four lines and his fifth are canon at 160:58–100 and are not repeated or improved on here.**

### STANDING DEFECTS, CARRIED AND NEW, WITH CHAPTER AND LINE

1. **Chapter 160:96 — `in four hundred and thirty cases they did not send it back`.** The arithmetic of the same sentence is **one hundred and sixty**, not four hundred and thirty. Carried from Batch 0001, not repaired, and **no chapter in this batch uses it.**
2. **Chapter 158:77 and 158:79 — `fourpence a load` … `That is eight shillings and eightpence`, for two hundred loads.** 200 × 4d = 6s 8d. Carried, not repaired, and **no chapter in this batch uses it. The three prices for a load in this county in Batch 0002 are the register's published three shillings, said at 168:77 and 170:21, the county's entered-claim rate of two shillings and eightpence at 163:29, and Tarin Keel's second load path, which is a road and not a carriage and is not quoted. This line previously gave 163:41 and 170:37 for the first of those, and 163:41 is a section break and 170:37 is a man putting a chalk down.**
3. **Sena Ollet's towpath and hours.** 151:77 and 155:3 and 160:140 give six miles each way, about two hours of light in the middle of it, and eleven hours up it. Eleven hours for twelve miles is consistent with a stop at a counter. **A Batch 0002 figure must not contradict it: 166:11 has a man walking the same six miles at the first bell and reaching the top of the district about the fourth bell, which is the outward walk only.**
4. **The count of people who know he cannot read is EIGHT, not seven, and this batch added one.** See the devices section and Finding 3 below. **CORRECTED by a review pass on 26 September 2026; the line read *SEVEN … and this batch adds none*, and Amos Kellock says it at 164:49.**
5. **Chapter 155:47 and 155:51 — the forbearance's age and the account's rate.** Fourteen days on day thirteen and £485 a year; both correct, about different things, and they may never be quoted together as one figure. **The batch adds a third frame that must never be joined to those two: 169:17's *about a year*, which is how long the carts have been on the account, and 169:29's *in the fortieth week of last year*, which is when they went on.**
6. **A RECORD ERROR AND NOT A PROSE ERROR: the volume's thesis sentence does not exist.** See Finding 2 above. **`state/continuity.md` (Batch 0001, *THE PEOPLE OF RYFOLD*, item 7) and `state/character-state.md` attributed *Nobody in this district is being cheated. Nobody in this district is being told anything* to Sena Ollet at 151:77 or 151:85, and a full-text scan of all one hundred and seventy chapters returns no instance of either half of it.** This batch did not install it in a chapter and did not leave the attributions standing.
7. **A FIGURE WITH NO CHAPTER BEHIND IT AND IT IS WRONG: the £2 3d difference.** See Finding 1 above. **`outline/volume-04.md` line 126, the Batch 0002 prompt's *THE MONEY* section, and the Batch 0001 sections of `state/continuity.md`, `state/batch-summary.md` and `state/open-threads.md` all asserted it. Chapter 169:17 states it correctly as two pounds. ALL FOUR STATE-FILE COPIES ARE NOW CORRECTED — the writing pass corrected none of them and a review pass found two of them still live, one of them in the same file as Finding 1.**
8. **Chapter 160:112's `three hundred and thirty-nine` and Chapter 170:21's restatement of it are the same figure about the same thing and are the Chancellor's own, out loud, in a yard, in Chapter 160.** Any batch that uses it must use it as his number and not as an independent count, because there is no other count of it in the world; it is what one man says he knows to the person because he keeps the register himself and has no clerk on it.
9. **THE TWELFTH PAGE AND THE ELEVENTH PAGE ARE NOT IN THE SAME CHAPTER AND THE ELEVEN PAGES DO NOT INCLUDE THE THREE SHEETS OF THE COAST, WHICH ARE FOUR HUNDRED MILES AWAY IN AN OFFICE THAT IS NOT LOCKED.** Volume 04 may not produce them and has not.
10. **NEW, AND IT IS THE SAME CLASS AGAIN: the size of the county's yards.** The chapters said **about nine hundred yards** four times — 163:43, 163:65, 163:75 and 167:41 — and **four hundred and thirty-nine yards** twice, at 162:33 and 167:59, and the two figures had never been reconciled by any pass. **The two instances of four hundred and thirty-nine have been repaired to about nine hundred in this pass, because nine hundred is the figure that carries the load-bearing line, and no state file ever quoted the number at all, so there was no record to be wrong in this instance. It is recorded here anyway, because the fact that a state file said nothing about a figure that was in the prose twice is itself the finding: an unrecorded figure is not a safe figure, it is an unchecked one.**
11. **NEW, AND IT IS A CRAFT DECISION AND NOT A DEFECT: `SIX CARTES. FOUR HUNDRED AND SIX.` at 163:99.** The boy of thirteen cannot read and has misspelled *carts* as it sounded to him. **It is deliberate, it is now marked as deliberate in the prose at 163:101, and it may not be corrected by a later pass.** It is the only number anybody in that building has ever voluntarily written down and it is in a misspelled hand, and the finding of Chapter 163 is that nobody can produce it.
12. **NEW, AND IT IS A COUNT IN THIS FILE THAT WAS WRONG TWICE IN ONE PLACE: the day-count of the counting-house visit.** Chapter 167 said *the seventh day of the forty-first week* and *Twenty-nine days*, Chapter 153 says *the seventh day of the fortieth week*, and the table puts the visit on Shelf day six and 167 on day thirty-seven. **The interval is thirty-one days. Both figures in Chapter 167 have been repaired, and *four weeks* has become *a month* at 167:75 and 167:85, and the state files that carried *twenty-nine days* in four places have been corrected.** See the intervals table under *THE CLOCK*.

### WHAT THE BATCH DID NOT DELIVER, AND MAY NOT BE READ AS HAVING DELIVERED

- **Stage 4, Burden Bearer.** He is Stage 3 and is Stage 3 at the end of Chapter 170, and none of the seven devices has moved. **Stage 4 arrives in Batch 0003 by a practice, a failure, a relationship and a permanent cost, and its permanent cost — a year of his own memory — is paid at the volume's climax, not when the stage is reached.**
- **The lease.** The tenancy ended on day twenty, the discretion runs to the cut, the renewal is two hundred and seventy-five loads payable in grain, and nobody has done anything about it. **Batch 0003 opens one day before the cut.**
- **The voluntary restraint clause nobody could write.** Still unspent, and it is still the thing the volume's remedy has to fail without.
- **The deposition cannot be withdrawn and cannot be contradicted.** 166:75 and 166:83. There is no second sitting for a forbearance this year; the next is the first of the forty-eighth week and it is for other matters. **The carrying of Ryefold as uncooperative is now on the account's own record and will be there in nine months when a clerk reads the bench's page, and nobody has asked for it to be off.**
- **The new question is not asked.** *Whose memory is missing from the account that records his payment* is not on the page in Chapters 161–170 and may not be.
- **Nobody was rescued, nothing was won, and the eleven yards of Volume 03 are not mentioned.**
