### Stage 2, Seam Reader — what it is and what it costs

- **Ilyan is now Stage 2, Seam Reader, and the stage is earned by a practice, a failure, a changed decision, and a named physical cost. He is not Stage 3 and gains no new party, no new authority, and no new remedy.**
- **What he perceives, inside a hearing field on a live covenant, is the assembly: which piece is bolted to which, and what a rule is resting on when it is resting on nothing with a name.** The effect appears as physical mismatch — two shadows from one body, a chain on a handbell trembling four seconds before it is struck, a gate that swings out toward the person the rule leaves out.
- **The limit, held in every scene: he sees how the rule is assembled, not why its authors wanted it.** A coherent rule can still be unjust, and the best-made instrument in the batch is unjust. His finding in the field is *it is well made; that is the problem; somebody made it well.*
- **It works on documents about money and not on a man.** He practised it on Kolm's entry and term four together, got a usable and true result in about four seconds, and paid for it immediately: his left hip went cold and for six seconds he could not have picked up a pencil.
- **He was wrong in public.** He read the seam and said the burden was on the gate hand, and the field showed him it was not: a burden that can be paid to a person is a burden somebody pays, and the word that let her be paid for thirty years was *ward*, and the seam went past her and down through a thing with no name at all. **The misassignment is what he carries, which is why the failure is in the body and not in the memory.**
- **The afterimage, named and persistent:** (a) the **left leg**, from the hip down, occupied by a forty-four-year-old gate hand's weight, gone by the fifth bell if he stands on a bank, back in a house with the door shut, gone again by the fourth bell; (b) the **low water in his nose in two layers**, the day over the night and the flat over the water, in a lane with hay in it, unresolvable to either; (c) **a chain on a gate moving in his right hand about four hundred yards away at the wrong end of the afternoon.**
- **The blackglass token is the same kind of thing as the afterimage.** It is a spent seal of a passage that has already happened. It has never been warm. It goes warm on a bank, and touching it takes the low water out of his nose for about fifty minutes, and it comes back with the water over the flat and a third layer under both. **Ilyan has understood that the passage and the afterimage are the same operation, that one was done to him without consent and the other is being done to him by the same somebody, and that the token is why he is here. He has not resolved it and he is not going to.**
- **Four people have heard him say it out loud** — Sera Quill, Sel Harrow, Sabin Roke (who wrote it down at the direction of the bench and was told nothing further), and Ansa Rell. **A fifth has it and never heard him say a word of it: Tarin Keel, who said the true sentence about it first, in front of sixty people, and was right.** **There is a sixth person who is the whole of it, who has not been told, who would not thank him, and whose afternoon is in his leg and is not going to be given back to him.**
- **Persistent effects accumulate until he rests, changes a decision, or shares a burden.** The accumulation reached the point in Chapter 58 where he could no longer look at a seam in a room without losing the leg, and **what it changed was not a speech: it was that he had to be taken off his feet by a route-arbiter and a boy of nine.**

### The new question, in the air and not resolved

- **The Bridgeward: a region on the far side of the water past the cut banks where two districts meet, with no district of its own, a toll taken for eight years, a keeper, a gate, a hut, and a woman of seventy-one called Wen Lask collecting it, her father before her and his father before him.** The road is good, the toll has never been short, the loads go through, and the book carries the ninth-bridge settlement copied out term for term with **eleven dead names entered as the hands against it**, because that is what the form's line is for. **Nobody can challenge it: a challenge requires notice to every party named on the form, and every party named is dead, and the clerk's office that took the form closed the week it went in, and the desk is a desk.** It works. **Bram Orren has written it in his line book, has put it in nobody's file, and has told Ilyan that chasing it is how you make a good road a bad road.** **Ilyan has not chased it, and has named the reason: the preservation office, and the price of going on after it, which is that records stop coming to everybody.**
- **The volume's climax is named in the air and not staged.** Ansa Rell's court has put to Mr Vale, in writing, the question **whether a district may pay a person who has no address**, sitting on the twenty-ninth, and the court's own first note is that it can write a clean instrument in one afternoon that will close the gate and be payable and unhouse nobody, and cannot write the other thing, and that **four benches have tried in nineteen years and produced eleven instruments that were valid and none that was affordable, and the court is afraid.** The court's second note: the missing thing is not a rule and not a remedy, **it is a piece of paper that a person can swear to in a room with no address in it, and no bench in this city holds one that travels.** **Ilyan's answer to the magistrate: the messy one exists, he wrote it once, it took a bridge and a guild and a child and a landlord and a clerk's slip, and nobody has ever written one for a ward, and he could not do it without a keeper.**

### New named people on the page

- **Wenna Tace**, 29, refuse hauler, the reader of the district.
- **Bryn Sallow**, 44, gate hand, thirty years, asked to be a name and not an office.
- **Corva Silt**, 31, clerk of the Halloway bench, holder of the print authority, and the district's own archivist of her mistake.
- **Nell Ambrey**, convenor of the Halloway bench, ~60, who raised the sanitation complaint and is losing it.
- **Cael Rundle**, 58, ratepayer of Weir Lane, whose daughter is fourteen. **She is not named in the prose and must not be named in state.**
- **Bream**, landlord of Weir Lane.
- **Abel Kearn**, 35, a builder out of the upper street, with a builder's apron and lime in the seam of it. He asks the batch's central question in Chapter 53 — *what did you do about this* — and lands its sharpest line: *then you are no use in this room.* Every other person in that room is named and aged, and so is he now. **He is a working man in a poor district who wants a gate that works and has no vocabulary for a charter; he is not a legal actor and must not become one.**
- **Oakes**, 34, Halloway's district maintenance clerk, who refused the wage in one sentence and was right.
- **Miko Ferren**, 9, of a house on the bank, who was told he was a trespasser by a carter and who does not want to be in the column.
- **Ansa Rell**, 44, magistrate, eleven years in the office. **Introduced in Chapter 55, per `outline/series.md`'s antagonist ladder rung 2.** She represents institutional authority and emergency clarity, she is right about the need for one legible rule, and her instrument would be procedurally valid and selectively burdensome. **She is not a villain and she is not defeated in this batch; her defeat belongs later and produces a difficult ally, not a redeemed servant.**
- **Dessin Nahl**, clerk of the Orison chamber, who wrote nine of the eleven names on the docket board.
- **Wen Lask**, 71, toll collector at Carrisand in the Bridgeward, named in a bell-runner's line book and not yet met.

### Other Batch 0001 facts

- **The ninth bridge and Halloway are four miles apart, and neither one knows anything the other one is doing.** The word *exception* travels faster than a charter and the batch's title is the reason.
- **Halloway's bench is a body, and the ward is not, and that is the whole of the difference.** A bench can pay; a ward cannot be paid; a person is paid in chalk.
- **The court has no power over the gate and says so on the face of the field twice.**
- **Odile and the court translator are the only people in this city who can read a legal instrument aloud to a room, and Sera Quill can read the guild's words. In a city of instruments they were used together in Chapter 55 and did not once disagree about a word, which happens about four times a year and did not happen at all in the first month of the case.**
- **Panel count for Batch 0001: three System-rendered entries, one each in Chapters 56, 57, and 59**, at most one in any chapter, and none stating a general rule. All three are formal records entered in Ilyan's open case: the field's opening and disputed words, the burden schedule as entered, and the challenge as filed. **They are set as plain bold lines with no blockquote marker and each is introduced by a narrative line, which is the Volume 01 convention and the only thing that lets a reader tell a panel from a piece of paper. Every block-quoted block in Chapters 51–60 is a physical object in the fiction.**
- **The flat's load count is a single sequence and it is Ilyan's: four hundred and six on the fourteenth, four hundred and nine on the seventeenth, four hundred and nine again on the nineteenth, four hundred and eleven on the twentieth.** It is the only number in the case that is his, it may be wrong by forty, and Tarin puts the twentieth-day figure to Dain as *four hundred and eleven loads, and who said yes to them.* **Any later batch that reports it must report it as one of those four or must say when and by whom it was counted.**
- **Word range for the batch: 3,028–5,305.** Chapter 56 is the longest and is not padded; it carries the field, the practice, the first use, the failure, the cost, and the token. **Do not treat either figure as a target.**

## Batch 0002 (Volume 02) established facts — Chapters 61–70

### The batch's map

- **Chapter 60 is the twenty-ninth day of the unloaded period. Chapter 61 is the same day, from the seventh bell. 62 is the thirtieth. 63 is the thirty-first and the second. 64 is the third to the fifth. 65 is the sixth to the eleventh. 66 is the twelfth to the fourteenth. 67 is the sixteenth. 68 is the seventeenth. 69 is the eighteenth and the twenty-first. 70 is the eighteenth to the twenty-ninth.** **No weekday is named anywhere in this batch or in any later batch without a reason.**
- **The props came off the fourth bridge on the twentieth of the first month — the twentieth day of the unloaded period, nine days before the hearing on the twenty-ninth — and stood under the ninth bridge's west seat for the fortnight that seat was given.** The seat's fortnight ran out on the **third of the second month** — the thirty-fourth day of the unloaded period, the ruling day counting as the first — and the span took its first load that day. The fourth bridge's deck has had **four square holes in it since the twentieth of the first month**; they were **twenty-five days old on the fourteenth of the second month**. **The props went back on the same cart on the eighteenth of the second month, twenty-eight days after they came off, twenty-nine counting both ends, and there is no figure of thirty-four days anywhere in this case and there was never one.** "A fortnight" is the length of the propping; "twenty-eight days" is the length of Anhalt's deck with nothing under it. **Do not move either.**
- **The review is the fourth morning bell of the twenty-seventh of the third month after the ruling, counting the month of the ruling as the first of the three. The second month of the case has thirty days. All three figures below are true at once and are the only three that may be used as the run to it: 49 days from the eighth of the second month (Ch 65), six weeks from the fourteenth (Ch 66), and four weeks — twenty-eight days — from the twenty-ninth of the second month, which is where Chapter 70 ends.** It is about fifty-nine days from the end of Chapter 60. **As a run to the review, "five weeks" and "seventy days" were both wrong and neither may be used to date it. That ban is narrow and it covers only that distance: "five weeks" is live elsewhere in the manuscript as ordinary elapsed time since Ilyan came over — the token in his pocket in Chapter 56, Nahl's gallery in Chapter 66, the bank in Chapters 69 and 70 — and those uses are canon, not errors to be tidied away.**

### The ward — the batch's structural finding

- **The word *ward* is a guild's word for a duty, not a place.** It is in the ninth bridge's term book under a bridge heading, *duties carried, not discharged*, with a star against it meaning somebody had complained about it: *ward. not a place. the work a district owes and has not got round to. a guild's word, old, from before the book is anybody's grandmother's. it means a thing you do, not a thing you live in.* It was found by Ilyan, who cannot read a clause, in a book that had been used as a weight under four benches in the rain, and it was corroborated in three places: **the standing of the wards, a hundred and ninety years old, in which eleven of fifty-five duties have been performed and forty-four have not**; **the traveller book's own front leaf at Halloway, one ward, five duties, none performed, the last performer dead thirty-one years**; and **Hanne Kolm's sworn volume, the one document in this case that does not use the word at all**, because the duties of a lower road are the ones a landlord actually does and nobody on that road calls them a ward.
- **The entered finding: a duty with nobody in it is not a duty nobody does.** A rate may be paid to a ward only if the ward is a body; the ward is not a body because it is not a person; a duty cannot be paid anything, it can only be given work; and the work goes to whoever is standing nearest. **This is why a woman stood at a gate for thirty years and why the word *ward* has let a duty be paid as if it were a wage.** The court has no power to make a seat, a name, or a body out of a piece of work, and Harrow said twice on the face of the record that the court has no power over the wards.
- **The two words this city has for the same poor — *the poor people* and *the carters, hauliers and gate hands* — are both wrong in front of witnesses**, and Abel Kearn, thirty-five, a builder, stood up and said he lives in the ward and is in neither of them. A count taken on the low water returned **five places**, and a place is the category the court came to get rid of.
- **Tarin Keel's count has no line in it: there is no way anywhere in this city to count the people who are not at the pump.**

### The two texts

- **Form L-3, the clean instrument, Halloway's, eight clauses, numbered, published at the fourth bell on the thirtieth.** Written in an hour and a half on the back of a weight receipt by **Bel Ordway**, convener of the weighbridge bench, a cart owner, not a lawyer, who signed it: *not a solicitor and not a drafter and not asked by the court. I wrote this myself and if it is wrong it is mine.* It closes the gate to loads in the district's direction at all times, opens it out of the district on the tide, says the gate is worked by the gate hand and by no other person, pays **four hands, four hours a day, by name, in chalk, out of the district's road funds**, sets a **water rate of a quarter of a penny a bucket** collected at the standpipe and not at a door, says the ninth bridge's *lawful traveller* does not apply to that gate and does not use the word, says a person on foot goes through and **may not** be refused, preserves the sanitation complaint, and expires at the third sitting of next quarter. **It was not made.**
- **The shared charter of the district of Halloway, nine sheets, five terms, made at the ninth bell on the seventeenth.** **The first four terms are copies of terms of the ninth-bridge instrument and are marked as copies. The fifth term is original and is about a person.** Read aloud on a bank in the mud to about a hundred and ninety people on the third of the month.
  - **The second half of the fourth term is the carrier's clause and it names Ilyan Vester**: the terms are carried to any district that asks, at his own cost and without pay, and **if he refuses, or is unable, or is not within four miles, the terms are not carried and the appeal is stayed until another carrier is sworn in that district, and nobody else may carry them.** Sera Quill wrote the consequence deliberately, entered that she chose it, and named the reason: a clause that cannot be broken is a post, and he had been a post for a month and it made him a gate.
  - **The fifth term is the holder: a person in the district who keeps the page and swears an entry made in it, and does not hold it for a district. A holder may not be a party and may not appeal.** **Wenna Tace, twenty-nine, the reader of the district, holds the page, in a room over a pump, and cannot appeal from it.** She was never asked; Harrow asked the question nobody had asked — *who in this room reads* — and she said yes, and then said what it costs, and then said the other thing: *it costs me the appeal and I am going to take it anyway, and put those two things in the same line.*
  - **Bream, fifty-four, landlord of Weir Lane, asked that the lane not be in the charter**, because every road in it runs to Weir Lane and the four rate-setters include two people who sleep there. Sera cut the lane out and told him what it cost: a district can be appealed about and a lane cannot, and a lane is where the people are. **The charter is worse because of it.** He said: *then it is a worse charter, I know it is a worse charter, I asked for it anyway, do not thank me and do not tell anybody at the pump.*
- **Both texts cost the water.** The clean instrument's rate is a quarter of a penny a bucket and the charter's is **half a farthing**, and the charter's is smaller only because the gate is shut and the cartage that feeds the district's road fund has been nil since the fourteenth. **Neither choice spares Weir Lane, and the choice is therefore not about money.**

### The money, in one place

- **The ninth bridge's district road fund is four hundred and ten pounds a year.** The fourth bridge's four props are ninety-six. **Two hands under a deck every change, at the ward rate, is two hundred and ten a year.** The remainder is **a hundred and four pounds.**
- **Meridith Vale spent four pounds on a cart and eleven pounds on two clerks' wages on the twelfth to tell Coldwater what it had adopted**, which leaves **eighty-nine pounds.**
- **The charter costs forty-four pounds and sixteen a year, and eleven pounds four in the quarter.** The paper is **two pounds seven shillings and threepence** — nine sheets at the district's own rate of three pence a sheet is two shillings and threepence a copy, printed in nine, sworn in one, and served in eleven, which is twenty-one copies — and it is the smallest of the three costs and is paid once. The keeping of the page is about a pound eleven a quarter for four hours in a week at the district's rate. **The serving of the notices is the largest of the three and is large because there is no clause anywhere in this city for serving a district, so a clerk is paid by the hour to work out who a district is, and the notices go twice in a quarter.** That quarter is the figure the rate on the pump is set to produce and not a unit more. **There is no figure of thirty-one pounds anywhere in this case; it was an arithmetic error in Chapter 64 and it has been corrected at source.**
- **The district could not buy four new props and did not: the old four went back on the same cart on the eighteenth for four pounds, the account is carried, and the deficiency of eleven pounds four in the quarter is entered against the district and not against the keeper of any span.**
- **Halloway's own road fund is two hundred and forty pounds a year, is fed entirely by cartage on a shut road, and cannot pay the charter at all. That is why the charter's money comes off the pump.**

### The appeal, heard

- **The first appeal in the record was served in four bells and two carts and it worked.** Fourteen names and one seal. **Corva Silt stood eleven minutes in a lane deciding whether a notice may be left with a person who has not asked for one, left it on the sill, knocked, did not wait, and wrote the time on the back in her own hand.**
- **The one seal was served on a desk with a stamp and no clerk and an incoming index that stopped four years ago, and nothing came back.** Dessin Nahl entered the date; the court took no view; nobody asked the court's witness about it.
- **Maren Kett, of the Orison chamber, nineteen years, was the named advocate**, because a claimant with limited standing may only be heard with one, and his first nine words were that he is not the man's friend. **He argued against his own client for an hour** and said on the record that he knew the outcome on the day he took the brief and took it anyway.
- **The disposition, in three parts.** (a) **The limit:** the ninth-bridge settlement binds no span but its own; *on this span* is the court's own drafting, is correct, and **is not widened**; the claimant knew it and filed anyway, in his own words, and nobody improved them. (b) **The receipt:** a review held at one span may be received by any bench in this city as a matter of record, on notice, and is not binding there, and a bench which has received it **may not enforce any part of it against any person unless it has adopted that part by its own act and its own minute.** (c) **The duty to answer:** a bench which has received the instrument **shall answer, in writing, in public, within eleven days, whether it holds any page, book, column, register, schedule, or sworn instrument which a person affected by a received record may swear to, in that bench's own room, at that bench's own counter, without leave. The court has no power to compel the answer and says so on the face of it.**
- **The eleven-day answers: nine of eleven answered.** Seven said they hold nothing. Two named a hundred-and-sixty-year-old document and then said on a second line that nobody can swear to it. **One answered *yes*** — a standing authority of preservation, which holds every instrument printed in this city for the last four years in a series running by plate, where any person may read any of it at a counter on a day, and **no person has ever asked.** One bench did not answer at all, and Rell entered that this is not a breach of anything.
- **The result, in Rell's words: a city has established as a matter of public record, in eleven hands, that it holds nothing a poor person can swear to. That is not a remedy and it is the first thing in nineteen years.**

### The choice, and what it cost

- **Bryn Sallow did not come to the hearing and the reason was entered first, in her own words**: the gate shall be worked by the gate hand and by no other person; if the gate hand is not at the gate the gate is shut; the gate being shut is a flat nobody has counted since the twentieth and about two hundred people; and she is not going to be the reason a court in Orison decides a thing about her in a room she is not in.
- **Nell Ambrey, convenor of Halloway's bench, entered the cost against herself**: the bench's time goes to appeals, the eleven-day answer, a rate, a serving and a holder, and her sanitation complaint is not heard this year, because it is in her gift only on a day when she has nothing else.
- **Cael Rundle voted for the clean instrument knowing his own lane's water and his daughter's bucket, and said so.**
- **Ansa Rell voted her own weight for the clean instrument, in writing, signed, for ever, and said the rest of the case out loud: it is the right answer, it is the correct answer, and a rule with no person in it is the exact shape of a thing that has no one who can be asked to say no. She was out-voted four to one against and is not defeated and not redeemed.**
- **Ilyan's contribution was nine words: *I am for the charter, and the reason is that a gate is not the problem.*** It was the only sentence in the room that a bridgekeeper could have said, and it was out-argued by his own advocate afterwards. He is not a party to either text and never asked to be and was not counted.
- **The charter was made with a person in its fifth term at the ninth bell on the seventeenth, and the carrier's clause stands with his name in it, accepted in nine words.**

### What it cost, on the ground

- **A rate of half a farthing a bucket at the standpipe, on a barrel lid at the gate side, in the district's own hand, the first tax in nineteen years, to be read aloud at the sixth bell on the first day of every quarter by the holder.** Cael Rundle said *this is the right rate* before he said anything else, and then said his daughter is fourteen and walks to that pump every morning, and that he asked for a line in a district book on which an hour of work could be paid to a person by name and was given a date, and that the line is full of another woman's name and the bucket is his.
- **The first appeal under the charter was sworn by Bryn Sallow against the office of gate hand**, on the first line of the schedule she wrote herself on the twentieth. There is no clause for serving a district and nine hours in a clerk's office found that you serve the person who keeps the district's book. **Corva Silt served her own district's appeal, minuted it, and entered that she printed H-9, that she has asked to be relieved of the print authority, that she is refused again, and that she is not relieved — and said she will not ask a sixth time.**
- **It was refused on the correct rule that a person may not refuse an office and keep it. And then the charter worked**, because four of its five terms are a bridge's: **Oakes set the district's own rate, a third of a haul rate, on the balance of the tide from the eighteenth, by name, in chalk, at the moment of the work, six and a half hours a day, and the thirty years before it is entered as owed by the district and not paid.** She said it is over a hundred thousand hours, refused to have them counted, and entered the cost herself: the four who set the rate include two people she buys salt off and has lent a bucket to, her refusal was entered before the rate was set, and the court entered the rate after the refusal, and the order is deliberate and is hers.
- **Bream said no again on the eighteenth, in a room over a pump, to the same question, before anybody had got as far as asking a different one: a roll says who owes him a shilling a week, and a roll is a page with a seal on it, and a page with a seal on it is a thing a bench can come back with. Nobody had asked him for forty doors. He swore a serving list of forty names standing, in twelve minutes, reading them out himself into a page the clerk held up and did not copy into the bench's book, and he read it over once more before the folder closed. He got one of the forty wrong — a man of nineteen who has paid a shilling a week since he was fourteen — apologised to him, and asked for the correction to be entered. It is the first thing he has ever sworn. The cost: forty people in a lane found out that their landlord's mouth had turned them into a page, and he is a body now, and if Weir Lane is ever contested he is the first name anybody will ask for. Nobody in the room offered him a shilling of the eight pounds a week that never came back, he did not ask for one, he asked for that to be entered, and it was.**
- **Corva Silt asked to be relieved of the print authority for the fifth time in six weeks and was refused and said she will not ask a sixth time.**
- **Wist Anhalt, forty-nine, keeper of the fourth bridge for twenty-nine years, was asked what he wants for the first time in a month, by Corva Silt, on the seventeenth, with two clauses in writing.** He said: not a page; the account carried; the eleven pounds four against the district and not against him; **the four words taken off the frame and put in a book on his desk, and the frame to say *fourth bridge, four words, asked and answered* with a date and nothing else**; and last, that he has been the man who was not on the bridge for twenty-eight days — the props came off on the twentieth of the first month and went back on the eighteenth of the second, and they propped the ninth bridge's seat for a fortnight and stood in a yard for the other fortnight — and would like that on a page in a book and not on a nail.
- **The clean instrument went back into a drawer in a weighhouse office with a note on the back in Ordway's hand: *made, not adopted. it is the best of the two and it is in a drawer, and a rule in a drawer is still a rule anybody can print.* The front is clean, because a rule that has a man's handwriting on the back is not a rule any more.**

### Stage 2 — what the practice has become

- **The afterimage does not clear when he rests. It clears when he is inside a burden of his own.** He had it exactly backwards for eleven days. It is a practice with a four-hour limit in it, discovered on a bank at the ninth bell, and **it is not a cure:** when he stops it comes back in about a minute and comes back worse, and it did not come back clear afterwards.
- **The counting:** six days of a gate hand's tide are on a slate with a line down the middle of it, in a load-carrier's figures, wrong by about four minutes in any hour, counted by a man and called by the gate hand with Wenna Tace holding the clock. **A person is not a measuring instrument and the court says so on the face of the schedule.**
- **The cost, named by the woman it belongs to:** she has got a number on a slate that only exists when a man from a bridge is standing on that bank, and he has made her thirty years into a job for a man who has a leg, and there is no line anywhere in this city that pays a man for having a leg. **His clearing is now load-bearing for somebody else's claim, and he cannot stop without making her hours a shape again.** Nobody is told about the leg but the four who already had it; the fifth worked it out and said it first; the sixth is the gate hand and she has not been told and would not thank him.
- **The token, reached once in this batch, on the sixth, on the sill-stone, in fifty minutes, and it cost him the question's only possible use.** The third layer resolved: the thing in his nose is not a memory of a low water and never was a smell; it is a **rendering**; and it is a picture of an oak gate seen from above and behind at about two hundred feet in a light with no source, wrong hinge, wrong leaf, wrong proportion, and **the same picture every time from the same height**, first seen in eighteen seconds on the edge of the flat at the first field on the nineteenth and mistaken for the low sun on the mud. **A rendering is made for somebody, and a rendering has a vantage, and a vantage is a place, and the place is not on that flat and is not in this city.** He does not know where it is. **The fourth layer, which came back under the other three, is a door in a wall at a distance at the same height and the same distance, and the vantage has not moved.** The Shale Mirror question is touched once and not resolved.
- **Ilyan's language limit cost him in the worst place it has yet cost him anything**: a hearing about a category, made of legal words he does not have, where he said out loud in front of eleven benches and a magistrate that he could not do it in the law's words, and it was entered, and Odile the court translator said it has happened four times in eleven years, that three of those were a man who wanted somebody else to do it, and that she had decided which this was was not her trade.
- **Panel count for Batch 0002: three System-rendered entries, one each in Chapters 61, 64, and 67**, at most one in any chapter, and none stating a general rule: the sitting as convened, the draft charter with no holder entered, and the disposition of the challenge. **All three are set as plain bold lines with no blockquote marker and each is introduced by a narrative line.** Every block-quoted block in Chapters 61–70 is a physical object in the fiction.
- **Word range for the batch: 3,761–5,380.** Chapter 68 is the volume's climax and is not padded. Chapter 69 was lengthened on review to stage Bream's reversal on the page; it is a scene, not an addition of fact.

### The new people, the second body, and the word on the form

- **Hulda Vane, 51, clerk of Coldwater, twenty-six years, a bench of five, three spans.** Copied the ninth-bridge settlement out of the district's book on the third of the month, lawfully, without reading it, on a sealed letter with no name on it. Read it after, alone, at night, and wrote a second clause into her own instrument so that nobody could do to her slipway what was done to a gate four miles off. **She is the mechanism again and she entered the truth about her own instrument every time she was asked.**
- **Coldwater's Form C-4, slipway lane, numbered, published at the fourth bell on the sixth of the month.** Clause two is the good one: no person not entered in the carter's book shall pass the turnbull with a load, and the word *traveller* is not used and not to be read into it. **Perdy Sill, 38, of a coal yard at the bottom of the slipway, is the template's second body and she wants a cart.** The instrument has made a man with a sledge four times dearer, correctly, and there is no word in it she can take hold of and shake. She ends by asking what a wheelbarrow has to do with any of it.
- **The Coldwater bench minuted in five hands what it had adopted and had not noticed, on the twelfth: nine terms adopted, term six not noticed and not adopted, term three not adopted in its effect, and *the ninth bridge's settlement is not binding on this district and was never binding on this district. we adopted it. we are not bound by it.***
- **The docket board had twenty-two names on it and eleven places**, and a name matter that first came up on the eighteenth was still on it at the end of the batch, having lost its place three times.
- **The word.** A form arrived in Halloway on the twenty-second of the month, printed in Orison in a district's typeface by a print shop filling a space in a docket, un-ordered, sealed and numbered, summarising the charter with an appeal in it, **with EXCEPTION at the top of it in a bigger letter than any other word on the page.** The court has no power over a form; a form is a habit with a seal on it. **The word is not in the charter, in the clean instrument, or in any instrument in this case, and it is in every clerk's drawer in nine districts within eleven days.** It came from a hearing, at the ruling, two months ago, where a recordwright said in the room it would be called an amendment and in a few days it would be called something else, and that nothing in the ten terms would stop a form being printed with that word at the top of it.
- **The Bridgeward, pushed once, by Bram, and answered.** He sent the charter's terms to **Wen Lask, seventy-one**, at Carrisand, on his own initiative on the eighteenth, and told a magistrate before anybody could stop him, and said the price: in about a year that gate is a gate a man from a city has written to about a rule, and that is the only reason anybody in that family would ever have heard of us. **Her letter came back on the twenty-ninth and it asks one question and gives none: *DOES YOUR PAPER SAY ANYTHING ABOUT A ROAD WITH NOBODY IN IT.*** The last name in the witness line is her father's, and she has written over it twice. Nobody answered the question. **The road is not damaged and the volume's new question is one step further along and no further.**
- **The continuity line appeared for the sixth time, on the twenty-second, on the copy kept in the holder's page, under the fifth term, and the word changed again: *CONTINUITY PRESERVES THE RECORD AGAINST THE HOLDER.*** Harrow refused for the **fourth** time, in the same words (*I will not rule on a line I did not enter*), entered it as an unentered line, named nobody, and said he would refuse on the seventh time. **Nobody in this case may name a responsible person and nothing may be named.**
- **Sera's two things, named in the last chapter and not resolved:** the quarter of a sentence on the facing leaf, which now has to be read as a duty and not a place, and **a district where a page is open to a person, which is not the same sentence and which she is not going to say it is**; and her family claim, six years old, unfiled, needing a page that may not exist, which she has not looked for in a month because she is holding a charter. The prohibition on the sealed preservation-index item stands.
- **The five things in the record that Ilyan cannot un-enter**, named with their sizes: two copies of a private sequence from *The Sovereign's Wake*; **the precedent** he asked for in a corridor; **the first appeal**, which worked perfectly and reached nobody who needed it; and **the carrier's clause**, which is the only one of the five somebody can call out and ask to see him, and which cost a district a day and a half of a cart.

## Batch 0003 (Volume 02) established facts — Chapters 71–80

### The batch's map

- **Chapter 70 is the twenty-ninth of the second month. Chapter 71 is the thirtieth. 72 is the first of the third month. 73 is the first. 74 is the second, the third, the fourth and the fifth. 75 is the sixth and the seventh. 76 is the ninth to the fourteenth. 78 is the fourteenth. 79 is the fifteenth to the eighteenth. 80 is the twenty-fourth to the twenty-ninth, with the public maintenance review on the twenty-seventh and the reading at the pump on the first of the fourth month.** **Chapter 77's own dates are the sixth, the seventh, the ninth and the eleventh of the third month, and then the fourth and the sixth of the fourth month, when the preservation authority answers the second question. The map entry that used to read "77 is the fourth to the seventh" was wrong and is not to be used. No weekday is named anywhere in this batch and none may be, in this or any later batch.**
- **The distances are fixed and must not be moved: the ninth bridge to Halloway is four miles; the ninth bridge to Nethergill is eleven miles; Halloway to Nethergill is seven miles; the Nine Yards are four miles beyond Lowgill bar and therefore about fifteen miles from the ninth bridge. The old weighhouse at Coldwater is on the road.**
- **The carrier's clause has been used exactly once in the fiction, on the thirtieth of the second month, for the district of Nethergill, and the consequence is visible every day.** The clause obliges a carrier to four miles. Past four miles it is a favour, and a favour cannot be entered, and he walked eleven miles anyway.
- **The token has now been used four times in the fiction. The fourth use took the words.**

### The first ask, and the two copies

- **The district of Nethergill**, eleven miles from the ninth bridge, a bench of four, a road fund nil for three years, a lane, no cartage. Jonas Pyle, sixty-three, convenor. Hollis Dune, thirty-seven, a drover's clerk, the district's only paid hand. Marguerite Skeath, fifty-one, who keeps the chapel book. The fourth member's name is not in this case and must not be given one.
- **Hester Vole, thirty-eight, of Nethergill**, a cowman's wife, the reader of that district, keeps the bar's tally in a book she has kept nine years. She can read a notice, a psalm and a tally and cannot read a contract. She has a boy of thirteen and two asleep upstairs. She was never asked and is not paid.
- **The ask.** Nethergill asked for the terms under the second half of the fourth term **and for the paper itself, and it cannot pay for the paper.** Four names and a mark and a wedge-and-chain seal nobody in the case had seen.
- **A copy of the charter came down the lane on a cart on the twenty-seventh of the month before the ask, brought by a drover, with no seal and no clerk, and it is SHORT of the second half of the fourth term** because a clerk does not copy a man's name into a district's paper when a district has not asked for a man. **Hester Vole found this, not Ilyan, by laying two sheets on a table.** The carrier's own copy was complete, which means a short copy of the charter was already in circulation in a district whose protection depends on that clause.
- **Four complete copies of the charter of the district of Halloway now exist: the holder's, in a room over a pump; the weighhouse office's, which binds nobody; the court file's, which is the copy the carrier walks with; and the one Nethergill swore out itself by candlelight in a kitchen on the first of the third month, read out twice at a pump by a woman of fifty-one, unpaid, which binds nobody and cannot be come back for.**
- **Nethergill wanted a person to come and stand in its room, and said so.** It did not want a charter, and it adopted nothing, and it said why: a bench cannot adopt a thing it has not read and half of what it was sent was not in the paper.

### The word on a form, and what it is

- **The finding of Chapter 72: the word *exception* is not a label on a rule. It is a claim that a rule is temporary, and a form that says so can be used against the person who relies on the rule.**
- **The charter of the district of Halloway has no end on it.** The clean instrument that lost (Form L-3) expires at the third sitting of next quarter, and the word is a correct Bench word for a temporary permission with a limit, an expiry and a burden on it, and it is old, and it is in nine clerks' drawers and is in no instrument in this case.
- **A form summarising the Halloway charter, with EXCEPTION at the top of it, is in a bench of four's table at Nethergill and in nine districts.** It is un-ordered, sealed and numbered. **A court has no power over a form.**
- **Hal Dreeve, thirty-four, of Nethergill, hires out barrows and lost a season of four months at a weighhouse, and a roof on his mother's two rooms with it.** **Parnell Slee, forty-eight, nineteen years at the weighhouse, is the person who refused him, and is honest: there were two reasons, and the second one is real, and the form gave him a sentence instead of *I do not know you*, and he asked for the shelf to be sent back.** The print shop filled a space in a docket and a foreman did not write a letter about nine forms in a fortnight.
- **Ilyan's language cost in this batch is a different kind of failure from Chapter 61: he can follow all of it and cannot act on one line of it**, because the charter is Halloway's, the form is a form, and the weighhouse is not a party to anything, and the rule that stops him speaking is a rule and it is correct.

### The toll bar, the Nine Yards, and a place that cannot have a page

- **Lowgill bar**: a stone, a slot with a stone in it, an iron chain with a ring, and a board painted once in guild colours saying toll on draught at a penny the load. **Nobody has worked it in living memory.** The first cart of the day lifts the chain. A waggon of hedge is not a waggon of draught, so it does not stop.
- **Rennick Hale, forty-one, a carter, took the pennies out of the slot into a tin under his seat for ten weeks, four pounds and six, and spent none of it, and asked in a room in Orison for it to be accounted for under his own name.** **The four pounds and six went back into the slot on the second of the fourth month, in a cart, with a docket in a clerk's hand, one penny at a time.** He is not a criminal and said so.
- **The Nine Yards**: sixty-one people, nine gates, one well with a bucket on a chain, no district, no bench, no clerk, paying Nethergill's rate for a hundred and forty-one years for a bar they do not own, because a rate followed a road and a boundary followed a hedge. **Merrin Coke, twenty-nine, keeps the count in charcoal marks on a shutter and is the only record anybody has.**
- **A matter came before the First Verdict against nobody**, with a carter as one party, a bench of four as another, and *the keeper of Lowgill bar* as the third, there being no keeper and none ever sworn in this city. **The place is a fact in the pleadings and is not a party, because a place is not a person.** Service was made by nailing the notice to the post, and the court accepted it, and **there is no clause in any instrument in this case for notice to a place.**
- **THE FINDING OF CHAPTER 79, entered in three hands in three places and the worst answer of the batch: a place can be given a page only by making a person a body, and the only person available is the carrier, and a holder is not a carrier, so the day he holds a page anywhere the appeal in the district of Halloway is stayed for about four hundred people.** There is no instrument in this city that says how a second carrier is sworn in. A holder may not be a party, may not appeal, may not appear in support of one, and may not be named in one. **The cost of a page for a place of sixty-one people is a door closing for everybody else.**

### The holder, and what she asked for

- **Wenna Tace is now the door for about five people a day in a district with nowhere else to send them, and the finding she entered is that a holder is consulted by everybody and named by nobody, and that it is one rule doing both things.** A person who needs a page sworn and cannot read one must find somebody who can read, and the only person in that district is the woman the paper will not have.
- **She asked a bench of four for a rate for a haul on the seventh of the third month and the cost of it is three-part and she stated all three before asking**: the rate will be set by the person who wants it; she must read it aloud at the pump at the sixth bell on the first of every quarter; and nobody in the district can appeal it. **A rate for a haul is three times the district's own rate, and the bench set it at the district's own rate, which is a third of a haul rate, so the haul rate in Halloway is two thirds short of a haul, and the hundred and fifty hauls of the last eleven months are entered as owed and not paid because the district has no money.**
- **The charter conspicuously has no clause about a barrow in it. That absence is Wenna Tace's, cut by her in Chapter 64, and it is now entered as the largest single want of the people the charter was read to, on the same page as the rate she asked for.** A second barrow is the want, and it is entered in her page for the first time in her life.
- **The page now has four entries.** Two from the eighteenth of the month before last, one on the sixth of the third month, one on the seventh.

### The debt, the figure, and the sixth thing

- **Oakes set a rate for the balance of the tide at a third of a haul rate, which comes out at a day's figure of nineteen and a halfpence for six and a half hours. The threepence an hour Ilyan used is not a rate this district has and never had.**
- **The figure, entered in the district's book on the tenth of the third month by a clerk in the district's own hand from a load-carrier's figures: a hundred and thirteen thousand four hundred hours at threepence the hour is one thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds and ten shillings. That is six years of the district's road fund and thirty-two years of the cost of its charter.** It is wrong three ways: the working tide of the turn is one day in seven and the week is about sixty-three hours, not seventy-three and a half, which takes out sixteen thousand two hundred hours and two hundred and two pounds and ten shillings; the rate is not a rate this district has; and thirty years of three hundred and sixty days is a number he invented. **Bryn Sallow gave the tide correction herself, unasked, and it is entered as hers.**
- **She refused the figure and was not compelled and cannot be compelled and cannot be overrule, and the refusal and the figure are on the same page in two hands.** The court has no power over the debt of this district.
- **The sixth thing Ilyan entered, on the fourteenth of the third month, named with its size: a figure of thirty years' work entered without consent, wrong by at least two hundred and two pounds and ten shillings and by an unnameable amount. He is now a witness in one more matter, and a witness can be produced. Nobody has thanked him and he did not ask.**
- **On the fourteenth Bryn Sallow wrote both sets of hours side by side, his figures and hers, four minutes apart in a four-hour day, and wrote under them: *TWO FIGURES, BOTH HONEST, BOTH WRONG A LITTLE. THE LINE DOWN THE MIDDLE OF THE SLATE STAYS. A PERSON IS NOT A MEASURING INSTRUMENT.*** The line down the middle of the slate stays.

### The public answer is a price

- **Dessin Nahl, clerk of the Orison chamber, walked eleven miles over six days and paid two of the six out of her own, and entered the price in the chamber's book in the same place as the answer.**
- **The district of Fallowditch**, a bench of three, eleven miles from the fen road, clerk **Perditta Nye, twenty-nine, three years in the post**, withdrew its answer of the eighteenth **in writing** on the third of the third month, and will hold a page from the fifteenth, and the reason entered in its own hand is a woman called **Adela Cray**, who asked for a place to put an agreement she holds and was told there was none and went home.
- **A bench that has said in public that it holds nothing has priced a room in its own district at one answer in eleven days.** In Fallowditch on the ninth of the month a landlord put the bench's own letter face up on a table in front of a woman of about forty with a fortnight's notice and an agreement she could not produce, and said he was not doing anything wrong, and read it out, which he did not have to, and did not want it known that he did.
- **Six of the seven *no*s answered, with reasons, and one said it would rather be a bench that can be honest than a bench that can be relieved, and nobody improved it.** The count, because it has been got wrong once: **of the other six, four gave the reason Fallowditch gave, one repeated the reason it gave the first time, which is the hundred-and-sixty-year-old book nobody may swear to, and one did not answer the question asked and answered a different one.** Four plus one plus one is six, and those five plus Fallowditch's own withdrawal are the six of the seven who answered with reasons. Three of the seven have been asked by a person in their own district to produce a page and have had to say, from their own letter, that there was nothing in their room to produce it from.
- **The standing authority of preservation was asked, for the first time in four years, to HOLD a page rather than to be read, and answered NO, in eleven lines, in the same office hand as its *yes* of the twenty-first of the month before, and for the second time in fifteen days: an authority that keeps records of persons is a bench, and an office with no person in it is not a door, it is a shelf. It can show that a rule was printed. It cannot hold a person.**
- **Nahl's own finding, in her own book and in no record: a city that has established it is empty has established that the one thing it is not empty of is a shelf; anybody who needs a page sworn can now name the one desk that said yes, and a bench cannot refuse that without being asked why it differs from the one body in the city that has said it holds everything printed here for four years; the leverage is real, it is on a desk with a stamp, and it cannot be used, and in about a year somebody will try anyway and there will be a form about it.** This is the nearest thing the next thirty chapters have to a finding and it has no power behind it whatsoever.
- **Twenty-two names, eleven places, unchanged in nineteen years. A place is made by a matter being ready and not by a name being on a board.** Three names went back on the board for a second time in three weeks, and one of the three is a man of nineteen who has been on and off it three times and has asked for nothing.

### The decision nobody could take back

- **Abel Kearn, thirty-five, a builder out of the upper street, put his name on the docket board on the eighth of the second month and rubbed it off with his thumb on the fourteenth of the third, in the open air, after telling the room what a name on a board is.** He refused to be heard. The board went to twenty-one names. **A name on the board cannot be put back on that board. The matter remains.**
- **His wall is a hundred and forty-one years old and its survey is in a book in Fallowditch, which has publicly said it holds nothing anybody can swear to.** The survey is in Fallowditch because the two properties were in Fallowditch's district a hundred and forty-one years ago and the boundary moved and the wall stayed.
- **A timber merchant three doors up stacked elm against it, is not a criminal, is right, took it off, and paid for the crack out of his own pocket and would not be repaid.**
- **His last question was six words: can I be counted as a place. Rell's answer: no.** A place is not a person, and a court that has just spent two months taking places out of its arithmetic cannot put one back in.
- **There is no clause anywhere in this city that allows a person to carry a sworn page four miles to a room. A matter is heard when a document can be sworn; a document is sworn where it is kept or in a room that holds it; a page is a record about a person; a person is the only thing a page is for.**

### The review, the twenty-seventh of the third month

- **The review was held, at the fourth morning bell, at the span, on public notice, in the open air, NOT called early and not postponed.** The span has carried a load at every change for fifty-four days; the band was inside it on a light load at mark and a half and on a heavy one at two and three, every figure taken with a moving cart; the pale permanent set has not moved.
- **The ninth bridge's district road fund, in Meridith Vale's hand: four hundred and ten a year. Two hands under a deck every change at the ward rate, two hundred and ten. The telling of Coldwater, fifteen. The charter, forty-four pounds and sixteen a year. The fourth bridge's four props, ninety-six. Remainder after all four: forty-four pounds and four shillings. Short of the props by fifty-one pounds and sixteen.** One hand under the deck is a hundred and five pounds a year. **TWO FIGURES AND THEY MUST NOT BE RUN TOGETHER: the charter's cost is forty-four pounds and sixteen, and the money left over is forty-four pounds and four shillings. They sit four lines apart in the same disposition and a speaker who has both in his mouth will garble one of them into the other. If anybody in any scene says the district has forty-four pounds and sixteen at the end of a review, that is the charter's cost and it is wrong.**
- **THE DISPOSITION: four new props are bought at ninety-six pounds out of the ninth bridge's district road fund; the four old props come out of the fourth bridge's deck and go into a yard; the deck of the fourth bridge is open in the weather until the new ones are in and nobody in the court can shorten that; the under-deck watch at the ninth bridge is reduced from two hands to one hand for this year; the count goes on at every change and is entered as what it is; the fifth-bell reading stays; the bell's wheel and pin are span maintenance and are inspected on a named day by two people, one of whom has pulled the rope; if the band is ever read outside the band the keeper has kept, by a name and with a date, the review is callable on the day it is read and is not waitable.**
- **T. Blore, twenty-two, eleven months at the west end under a deck, loses the second hand. He is the only person in this case's record named as having lost something in it, and it took a defendant with limited standing to put him there.** An apprenticeship in that trade wants two seasons of a span and one is not two. **What the guild actually takes off is a half day, and a half day is four hours, and four hours a day is the figure in every line about him, including a magistrate's — Rell says *four hours a day* and must not say *an hour*.** Oren Sedge is the man who will tell him and is not the man who took it. **Sedge has NOT told him, on the twenty-seventh or the twenty-eighth or at any point in this batch, and he commits to no date at all: he says it will not go in a letter, because a letter from a trade comes at the change and a man on a deck will read it standing up and take it for a summons about something else, and that he will say it at the second bell the first morning he can get down to the cut banks and stand in front of the man.** Nobody has written to him. **The telling is Batch 0004's, and as of the first of the fourth month he does not know, which is a live state and not an oversight — do not have him informed in a scene, do not have Sedge send a cart, and do not pin a date to the telling in a piece of state.** **The guild is out of money, the district has forty-four pounds and four shillings left in the year, neither is a person, and neither has said anything to him yet.**
- **Meridith Vale entered that the court did not ask the keeper of the fourth bridge and takes no view.**
- **Ilyan was produced in person with his report and the two copies of his own sequence, read both out loud to about ninety people, and named who paid: Bryn Sallow, Wenna Tace, Bream, Wist Anhalt, Corva Silt, Nell Ambrey, Cael Rundle, Abel Kearn, Hester Vole, and Tam Blore first of all.** He did not read the six things again, because a thing read twice is a thing being made bigger.
- **Harrow invented his THIRD procedure in public and named it as his own** — the numbering was corrected by the Volume 02 audit, because Chapter 80 line 122 calls this one the fourth and Chapter 91 line 94 and Chapter 100 line 22 also call something the fourth, and both cannot be the fourth: **where an instrument refers to another carrier being sworn in a district and there is no person sworn in that district, any bench in that district may swear in a carrier on the application of a person of that district, in its own room, in public, on a day the bench appoints, and the name shall be entered in the bench's own book with a date, and swearing in a carrier is not an adoption of the instrument in any other district.** The court has no power over any such bench. It is not precedent. **The full sequence of his four: Chapter 39 first (a reopening sets aside a record, a scheduling sets a day), Chapter 43 second (the burden of each remedy said by the person who objects to it), Chapter 80 third (this one), Chapter 91 fourth (the court's own motion that a question put to a person by post from a district that has entered it in its own book is not of itself a matter, which Sera Quill refused, and which Harrow himself called the worst of the four).** **The continuity line's refusal and his unentered-line entry are expressly not procedures and are not to be counted as procedures.** **It is not a remedy: a right that costs a district money, has no standing outside one bench, and depends on a man of thirty-one being within four miles is a favour with a seal on it, and the procedure cannot be used by anybody, because the only district with a carrier is the only district that does not need one.** That last sentence is in Nahl's own book and in no record.
- **The continuity line appeared for the SEVENTH time, on the copy in the record of the public review, under the fifth term, and Sel Harrow refused it for the FIFTH time, in the same words, and named nobody, and said he would refuse on the sixth and the seventh.** **The holder has still not read the line under the fifth term of the copy in her own room, and in Chapter 71 she says why: a charter ends at the fifth term and there is nothing after it, and she reads a charter in order. She has asked him not to read it to her. Nobody will.**
- **On the first of the fourth month the holder read the quarter's rate at the pump: half a farthing a bucket, six and a half hours a day in chalk with a name against them, the thirty years entered as owed and not paid, the figure of one thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds and ten shillings with the line that says it is wrong, and a rate for a haul at three times the district's own rate, two thirds short, unappealable, arrears owed and not paid.**

### Stage 2, and the token's fourth use

- **The afterimage is unchanged in kind: it clears inside a burden of his own and does not clear when he rests, it is a four-hour limit, and it is load-bearing for somebody else's claim. The clearing held on eleven miles of walking, at the cost of the practice being a favour past the fourth mile and the leg stopping forty yards past the end of a lane.**
- **He took a barrow-load of a stone off a chapel flat with a woman of fifty-one who did not ask him, and the cost is in the body and cannot be entered anywhere: his left hand opened in four places and is a scar at the base of his thumb in four years. There is no line anywhere in this city that pays a man for a hand, and there is none that pays a man for having a leg.**
- **THE TOKEN, fourth use, on the second of the third month, on a chapel flat, in fifty minutes. It took the WORDS.** From the second of the month until the sixth he could see a line of writing and could not get sense out of it. He told Corva Silt *I cannot read that* at a counter with four sheets on a table, and she sat down and put the pen down and did not enter it. **Corva Silt is now the second person in the world who knows that Ilyan Vester cannot read, and it is not on a page, and she named the four roles he is entered in and said she is not going to be that second head for free and is not going to refuse either.** Recovery: figures on the fourth, words in a room on the sixth, words in the rain on the seventh.
- **The rendering resolved one step further and is NOT resolved. It has a floor. The vantage is a room: boards, a table, a lamp with the wick down, a window with weather on the outside of it that is not this weather, a chair, and four people at the table, one of them about forty-four with her back to him and her right foot up on something. He looked at her for nine seconds and knew the shape of it and knew he had never seen it. He has been inside a rendering of a gate for a month and the gate is the subject, not the place. The vantage is at the same height and the same distance it has always been, the table is the size of a table you could put a hand on, and he is not going to explain that.** The Shale Mirror question is touched and not resolved.

### The Bridgeward, pushed once more, and the volume's new question

- **Wen Lask's question was read out at a public review on the twenty-seventh of the third month and answered by the magistrate, and the answer is entered as not being the answer:** the terms say something about a person and nothing about a road, and this court has no power over a road in a region with no district and takes no view and has said so twice. **The road is a good road, the toll has never been short, the loads go through, four hands are paid on the day and their names are written in the book by a woman of seventy-one herself, and the last name in the witness line is her father's and she has written over it twice and does not know why she keeps doing it.** The road was not touched. Nobody went and looked and nobody is going to. Bram's price for the first push was minuted; the court was not annoyed and entered that the not-being-annoyed is the closest this case can come to an admission and is not one. **This is the second push in the volume and it is the last.**

### Panel accounting for Batch 0003

- **Four System-rendered entries, one each in Chapters 71, 78, 79 and 80**, at most one in any chapter, and none stating a general rule: the second half of the fourth term and what the record does not show; the docket of the First Verdict and what a name on it is; the notice served in a matter in which no respondent is a person; the disposition of the public maintenance review. **All four are plain bold lines with no blockquote marker, each introduced by a narrative line.** Every block-quoted block in Chapters 71–80 is a physical object in the fiction. **There is no bold used as emphasis in running prose or dialogue anywhere in Chapters 71–80: a rendering is plain bold, a document is block-quoted and bold, and there is no third category.** Three passages carry bold inside a narrative sentence, and all three are a text being reproduced word for word rather than a stress on a word — Chapter 77's question as it is put to the authority, Chapter 80's one name-line as Roke enters it, and Chapter 80's closing question in a clerk's book. **They are not to be stripped, and they are not a licence to add emphasis.**
- **Word range for the batch: 3,269–6,678.** Chapter 80 carries the review and the batch close and is not padded. Chapter 77 is the shortest and is a complete scene. **Do not treat any of these figures as a target.**

## Batch 0005 (Volume 02) established facts — Chapters 91–100. THE VOLUME'S RESOLUTION

### The batch's map

- **Chapter 90 ends on the second of the fifth month in Orison. 91 is the second to the fourth and ends at the first bell of the fifth, when she goes down the counting-house lane; 92 is the fifth, from the fourth bell in the morning. 93 is the sixth to the eighth. 94 is the ninth to the twelfth. 95 is the thirteenth to the sixteenth. 96 is the seventeenth to the twenty-first. 97 is the twenty-second. 98 is the twenty-fourth to the twenty-eventh. 99 is the twenty-eighth of the fifth month to the twenty-eighth of the sixth. 100 is the first of the seventh, at the fourth morning bell in a city and at the sixth bell at the low water.** **No weekday is named anywhere in this batch and none may be named in any later batch.** **The month lengths the run above depends on, each fixed by a canon chapter: the first month has thirty-one days, the second thirty, the third thirty-one, the fourth thirty-one, the sixth thirty.**
- **The distances are fixed and were not moved: the ninth bridge to Halloway four miles; the ninth bridge to Nethergill eleven; Halloway to Nethergill seven; the Nine Yards about fifteen from the ninth bridge; Fallowditch eleven miles from the fen road; the cut banks nineteen miles; and a building with no name on the door, four miles from the court, holding the standing authority of preservation and a cold room of nineteen shelves.** The distances in this batch are the ninth bridge to Halloway four, Halloway to a district of a bench of three seven, Halloway to Coldwater four days up the fen road, and Coldwater to a bridge four days up the fen road.
- **VOLUME 02 IS COMPLETE AFTER CHAPTER 100.** The two resolution lines in `outline/series.md` are delivered: the appeal survives and is not secured, and Sera Quill has recovered a fragment of the original Quiet Chamber terms.

### The fragment, exactly as it is entered

- **Entered under licence on the twelfth of the fifth month, in a cold room in a building with no name on the door four miles from the court, upon the facing leaf beside the empty slot and NOT upon the removed page, which is not on the shelf and has not been on it since before she was born:**
- **THE READABLE PART OF THE SECOND SHADOW ON THAT LEAF IS: [  ] OF THE ENTRY IS A DUTY OF THE WARD, AND [  ]** — **ten words** (count the content words and nothing else: OF THE ENTRY IS A DUTY OF THE WARD AND), about four inches of shadow, one inch of it lost under a crease in the leaf. **Entered as one quarter of a sentence. It is not the clause. It does not restore any right of anybody to answer no.** **The number nine does not belong to this fragment anywhere in the manuscript or in this record. The printed form in the same building gives away eleven words and the leaf gives ten; the leaf and the form are never the same length and Chapter 94 says so on the page.**
- **The first line of the removed page still stands where it stands, entered on the twentieth of the second month, beginning with a lower-case *a*, and was not re-entered.** The already-known public fragment *the governed may answer no* is still not part of it and is still not on the facing leaf.
- **The comparison of the twenty-fourth of the second month — the facing-leaf line and the page over a pump, side by side, on the same page, with the statement that they are not the same sentence — STANDS UNAMENDED.** She writes that one quarter of a sentence more does not make a better comparison, it makes a longer one, and that a reader in twenty years may prefer the earlier entry and would be right to. **The batch did not top Chapter 70 and says so on the page.**
- **Read as a duty and not as a place.** A ward is a duty and not a place (canon, the guild's term book, a hundred and ninety years old). A duty cannot be answered, it can only be given work, and the work goes to whoever is standing nearest. **In four miles the person doing the keeping of the entry is Wenna Tace, the holder of the fifth term, who cannot appeal from one word of it and who has never been told.**
- **The answer to the holder's question of the twenty-ninth of the fourth month is a consequence and not a remedy:** a page is for keeping the record of a duty, this district has never written down a duty in any book at any time, and the oldest thing she has ever read does not know her name.
- **The removed page and her duplicate remain two events on two dates.** One was cut out of a bound volume before she was born and a mark in a newer hand is beside the slot; the other was a duplicate in her own hand in a case in her own rooms and is gone with no mark and no cut thread. **The reason they are not one event is entered twice in her own book with both dates on it, and the reason she wants them to be is entered as the only thing she has wanted in six years that costs nothing to hold.**

### The counting-house, and the man in it

- **THE STANDING AUTHORITY OF PRESERVATION IS IN A BUILDING WITH NO NAME ON THE DOOR AT THE LOW END OF A STREET OF COUNTING-HOUSES, FOUR MILES FROM THE COURT. THE DOOR IS NOT LOCKED. THERE IS NO CLERK. THE INCOMING INDEX STOPPED FOUR YEARS AGO. THE COLD ROOM AT THE BACK HAS NINETEEN SHELVES AND IS OPENED BY A MAN WITH A KEY.**
- **CORRIN VASK, FORTY-ONE, HAS SWEPT THAT BUILDING FOR NINE YEARS, IS PAID NINPENNENCE A DAY, OPENS THE COLD ROOM, AND HAS NEVER BEEN ASKED A QUESTION ABOUT WHAT IS ON THE SHELVES.** He said so out loud, unasked, on the fifth of the fifth month. He was asked one on the twelfth, and he spent it on the only question in this case that has no answer. He is right about the shelves and wrong about what being asked would make him: knowing where a thing is is not the same as being able to say what it says, and he knows that now and is right about it.
- **HIS RIGHT HAND IS SIX YEARS GONE AT THE WRIST AND HE COULD NOT OPEN IT AFTER THREE HOURS WITHOUT DOING IT ONE FINGER AT A TIME. He held a four-hundred-year-old leaf flat with the heel of it for three hours a day over four days.**
- **THE PRICE OF OPENING A VOLUME IS A SHILLING, TAKEN OUT OF A DRAWER, IN NO MINUTE, ON NO BENCH'S ORDER, AND THE PRICE IS ENTERED IN THE BUILDING'S OWN DAY BOOK IN THE SAME COLUMN AS THE SWEEPING OF ITS FLOORS, WITH THE NAME OF THE PERSON WHO PAID IT.** The sweeping of the days a volume is open is entered as not done, and there is one man for the floors and he is the man with the lamp. **A thing with no price in it looks like a service, and a record that a reader cannot leave until a person has paid for it is a door, and this building is not a door.**
- **ON THE FOURTH SHELF COUNTING BACK FROM THE WINDOW THERE IS A BOX** that came in on a cart in the winter before last with a bench's seal and a docket, never called for, dusted about two hundred times: **about nine hundred and forty forms, all one print, all one shop's typeface, with the word EXCEPTION at the top of each in a letter bigger than any other letter on the page.** He has read the word about four hundred times and does not know what it means, and **he would not tell her the word because she had not asked him that one, and he wanted one question asked of him for nine years and had had about four.**
- **The preservation authority's series holds only four years of print.** It was asked three questions about paper and one about what a thing is for, and answered the first three yes and the fourth no with a reason, in the same office hand, three times. **Sera Quill has decided not to ask that desk a fourth question, and has entered what it cost her: the only desk in the city that said yes has now said yes and no and a reason three times, and a person who needs a page sworn can no longer even point at it.**

### The three printings, and what is on the form

- **THE TERMS OF THE QUIET CHAMBER WERE NEVER PRINTED IN THIS CITY. WHAT HAS BEEN PRINTED ABOUT THEM, THREE TIMES IN FOUR YEARS, IS A FORM FOR LODGING A MATTER IN THAT CHAMBER, made by a shop filling a space in a docket, in a district's typeface, un-ordered, sealed and numbered.**
- **The first printing is the oldest and the shortest. The middle printing is the only one of the three with words in the fourth line from the foot, and it reads, entire: AND THE KEEPING OF IT IS A DUTY OF THE WARD. In the first and the third that fourth line is an empty space with a rule under it.** The first and the third were copied from the first, in that order. **A thing copied from a thing copied from a thing loses whatever was not in the pen.**
- **The word *the keeping of it* in the middle printing is a word the copy-holder made up**, because the line on the leaf begins with a subject the form could not read to the end, and a form has to name its subject. It is a true line and it is eleven words long and it is the truest line on the sheet, and it gives Sera two of the characters the crease takes, and **she did not use it.** A form is a copy made by a person who had to leave a volume. A copy is not a witness. A witness is a person and may be examined; a form cannot be examined by anybody ever.
- **This is a marked echo of a canonical sentence by a different person and it is not a recovery. It is a thing about paper and not about a person, and it is on a shelf four miles from a court in a case in which nobody has ever asked for it.**

### What a load is, and what it is worth

- **ON THE TWENTY-SECOND OF THE FIFTH MONTH A BENCH OF FOUR AGREED, IN ITS OWN BOOK, IN ITS OWN HAND, THAT A LOAD IS A BARROW**, and that the four in the guild's sentence is a fact about a cart and a cart does not come. **The rate for a haul set on the twenty-eighth of the third month is not amended, not withdrawn, and stands as sworn, and the two do not agree with each other, and the bench has entered that they do not agree.**
- **The bench did not agree what a load is worth, and entered why: the price of a load is the same question as the price of a tide, and the number of loads in a working tide is not known to the bench, and the only person in about two hundred and forty people who knows it is the person who hauls them, and the holder may not be asked in a support of anything and may not be named in one, and the bench asked anyway, in public, with her name on her answer.**
- **HER ANSWER, ENTERED IN THE BENCH'S OWN BOOK AND TO BE READ OUT FOUR TIMES A YEAR FOR THE REST OF HER LIFE: there is no day in this district that is a day.** She is now a woman in a bench's own book who has said out loud, in public, that her own work has no day. **A unit is not a price.**
- **The two debts are now in one unit for the first time in four months: the hauler's hundred and fifty hauls are a hundred and fifty loads, and the gate hand's six thousand hauls are about six thousand loads, and the guild's four-loads-to-a-cart figure is not used for either because it is a fact about a cart and a cart does not come. Neither is in any coin and neither can be. The figure of one thousand two hundred and fifteen pounds stands, because a bench of four cannot strike a number a witness entered and the woman it is owed to refused it and was not compelled and cannot be overrule.**

### The money, one step further

- **Meridith Vale, clerk of the ninth-bridge's district, nineteen years in the post, walked four miles on the twenty-second of the fifth month and paid his own way, and entered it.** The charter costs forty-four pounds and sixteen a year out of four hundred and ten, the year as set out at the review leaves forty-four pounds and four shillings, and the charter costs twelve shillings more than what is left, and the hundred and five pounds in the under-deck line cannot be moved into the charter's line without a minute of his own bench.
- **A fund is a list of lines and a clerk writes them in the order of what they buy, and the rule is about a hundred and forty years old.** In the spring, when the ninth bridge's year is set again, **he will write the charter of the district of Halloway below the hand under the deck, because he cannot put a room above a body.** He said so in advance, with a date, having been asked by nobody, and he refused in advance and in public a minute of his own bench that would let a bench of four four miles off ask for the hundred and five.
- **The person to be angry at in this case is named and is doing something defensible.** The price of a poor district's law in the spring is the price of a hand under a deck, and that hand is a man of twenty-two nineteen miles off who is not named in the charter and is not a party and has lost four hours a day.
- **The court has no power over any rate in any district in this city.** It said so on the face of a field about a gate in the second month and has now said it about money, and it is correct.

### The form, and the pitch at the bottom of the slipway

- **THE FIRST DISTRICT TO PRINT THE TERMS OF ANOTHER DISTRICT'S CHARTER ON A FORM HEADED WITH THE WORD IS COLDWATER, a bench of five with three spans.** Hulda Vane, fifty-one, twenty-six years in the post, ordered four hundred and sixty-three of them herself out of an Orison shop, from a correct sum: **a form is a shilling and fourpence against thirty-one clerk's hours a quarter of serving notices by hand, because no clause in this county says how to serve a district.**
- **She read the word before the print came back, thought about it for the better part of a minute, and printed it anyway, twice more, and has entered all of it in her own hand including the parts that are against her.** The reason is right and the sum is right and the print shop filled a space in a docket and a foreman did not write a letter about nine forms in a fortnight.
- **PERDY SILL, THIRTY-EIGHT, OF A COAL YARD AT THE BOTTOM OF THE SLIPWAY, WHO HAS WANTED A CART FOR ABOUT TWO YEARS.** Her district's own Form C-4 has one good clause in it and clause two is the only thing keeping the lane open. A man with a sledge produced her own bench's form in the office in front of five people and said the clause in it is under a word that says the whole thing is temporary and asked for her pitch back in writing, in a month. **She is right about six of her nine sentences and wrong about the rest and she gave the pitch back, and her ninth sentence is that she would still be in that lane if the word was not on the form.**
- **THE FINDING OF THE TWENTY-FIFTH OF THE FIFTH MONTH: a form is a habit with a seal on it; the word at the top of one is a claim that a rule is temporary; and such a claim does not take the clause.
