- **Chapter 98, line 45: the form arithmetic does not close.** 400 ordered + 320 ordered = 720; 463 held + 140 in the shop's drawer = 603; 117 unaccounted. **463 is load-bearing — it is the eighth thing Ilyan entered and it runs through Chapter 100. Do not restate the order figures.**
- **Chapter 99, line 5: "A clerk of thirty-eight"** — Perdy Sill is thirty-eight and of a coal yard and is not a clerk. The volume's convention is *a clerk of twenty-six years* (Hulda Vane) and *a clerk of thirty-one* (Corva Silt).
- **Chapter 99, line 77: "has not been in this case since the twenty-fifth of the fourth month"** — contradicted by Chapter 96, earlier in the same volume.
- **Chapter 80, line 122 vs Chapter 91, line 94: two procedures both called the fourth.** Corrected in the Batch 0003 section above.
- **Chapter 70, lines 79 and 87: the refusal in Chapter 70 is called the sixth.** It is the fourth. Four later chapters on later dates are consistent only with the fourth. The appearance count (sixth) and the refusal count (fourth) were conflated.
- **Chapter 59, line 61: "twelve miles"** contradicts "four miles" in the same speech and the canon distance.
- **Chapter 98, line 99 and Chapter 89, line 81: two month names one month out** — "In March" for an act Chapter 70 dates to the twenty-second of the second month, and "in March" for a vote Chapter 68 holds on the seventeenth of the second month. **January is the first month, February the second, March the third, April the fourth, May the fifth, June the sixth, July the seventh; the calendar reconciles with the numbered months everywhere else.**
- **The water rate's start day is given two ways** — the eighteenth in Chapter 69's narrative at line 3 and on the board's own printed text at line 5, the seventeenth of the second month in Chapters 87, 88 and 100. Say "the second month" and do not pick a day.
- **Chapter 92, line 169: "ninety days"** is ninety-four from the third of the second month and ninety-one from the sixth. Treat it as Ilyan's own count and build nothing on it.
- **Chapter 92, lines 119 and 137 against line 145: a hand that cannot open is "still open on the table"** eight lines later.

**Also corrected in place, from figures the chapters do not support:** the second carrier's-clause ask cost *a day and a hedge* (Ch 85), not four shillings and sixpence; the third production cost *three shillings and ninepence and a day and a half* (Ch 99), not four shillings and sixpence; four shillings and sixpence belongs to the appeal notice in Chapter 96. **Two chapter titles in `state/chapter-summaries.md` did not match the prose headings and now do.**

## Power and failure constraints

No respawns, save points, character replacement, or unearned level-ups. A failed ruling remains consequential. A burden must be named and may not be silently assigned to an unwilling person. Death is permanent. Ilyan grows through practice, failure, relationships, changed decisions, and permanent costs. A Foundation Challenger may open a blank interval only with notice, consent from people remaining in the full spill zone, and safe withdrawal margins; the final use permanently costs Ilyan the lived memory of filing the *Sovereign’s Wake* refusal report.

## Ending constraints

The final adversary is the First Witness already introduced in the Bench. Ilyan must reject the Final Exception even though it will work, give up permanent authority and a personal return route, and help create distributed regional charters. The final image shows an ordinary person refusing a specific rule with visible consequences and remaining part of the community. No new cosmic threat is planned.

## Deliberately closed or held — do not advance

- The historical right of refusal. **The right of anybody to answer no is not restored. The fragment is ten words with a crease in them, entered as one quarter of a sentence on the twelfth of the fifth month, and it is not a recovery of the clause.**
- The authorship question and the Shale Mirror.
- The responsible person for the C—2 chain and the standing preservation authority. **Ilyan has said out loud three times that he is not going to, most recently in a lane on the sixteenth of the fifth month.**
- The first witness behind the fifth mark in the count book. Ilsa's grandmother, the woman paid off the load book, and the levy ledger's cut name remain three separate things and Ilsa has still not been asked to decide.
- The Quiet Chamber's original page and Sera's duplicate remain two events on two dates.
- **The Bridgeward's toll and its dead witness list. It is now been put as a question about a person, out loud, on a mud bank, by a boy of seventeen, and it is not answered. It is not to be investigated in a way that damages a road that works.**
- The materials store and the Ro household's ground floor.
- The plate and the older witness index are one document cut once — a result, not a fresh clue.
- **The fifth mark in the count book, the preservation index, and a plate-and-index comparison are all used and closed. Do not restage any of them.**

## Batch 0004 (Volume 02) established facts — Chapters 81–90

### The batch's map

- **Chapter 80 ends on the first of the fourth month at the sixth bell at the low-water standpipe. Chapter 81 is the second of the fourth month. 82 is the third. 83 is the fourth to the sixth. 84 is the sixth to the ninth. 85 is the tenth to the thirteenth. 86 is the fifteenth. 87 is the sixteenth to the eighteenth. 88 is the nineteenth and the twentieth. 89 is the twenty-fifth. 90 is the twenty-sixth to the twenty-ninth, and then the second of the fifth month in Orison.** **No weekday is named anywhere in this batch and none may be, in this or any later batch.**
- **The distances are fixed and were not moved: the ninth bridge to Halloway four miles; the ninth bridge to Nethergill eleven; Halloway to Nethergill seven; the Nine Yards about fifteen from the ninth bridge; Fallowditch is eleven miles from the fen road and four miles from its own farthest place, and about two hundred and forty people live in it.** A bridge **four days up the fen road** and a weighhouse may be referred to without a number.
- **The water rate was chalked on the barrel lid in the second month, when the charter was made, and the first full quarter's reading was the first of the fourth month. THE START DATE IS GIVEN TWO WAYS IN THE CHAPTERS AND THE AUDIT DID NOT RESOLVE IT, BECAUSE THE CHAPTERS ARE CANON: Chapter 69 line 1 says the rate went on the board "on the morning of the eighteenth" and the board's own printed text says "SET BY THE BENCH OF FOUR ON THE EIGHTEENTH OF THE MONTH", while Chapters 87, 88 and 100 date the rate to "the seventeenth of the second month". Say "the second month" and do not pick a day. Every reference in Chapters 81–90 says the seventeenth of the second month, or *last month* for the twenty-eighth of the third month, and those two are the only forms.** The haul rate was set on the **twenty-eighth of the third month** and can be called the twenty-eighth of last month from the fourth month. **The next quarter's reading is the first of the seventh** and is better than two months away from the sixteenth of the fourth.

### The money, and the bill that was never sent as a notice

- **Meridith Vale's letter of the second of the fourth month is the chapter's engine and it is a bill, not a notice, and no reply is asked for.** The charter costs **forty-four pounds and sixteen a year** out of the ninth bridge's district road fund of **four hundred and ten**, a little under a ninth. **The year as set out at the review leaves forty-four pounds and four shillings, so the charter costs twelve shillings more than what is left, and they will pay it twelve shillings over the line.**
- **THE LINE THAT MATTERS: one hand under a deck is a hundred and five pounds and came back on the day of the review, and it is in a different line from the charter, and a clerk cannot move a line into another line without a bench's minute. So the ninth bridge's district has a hundred and five pounds it cannot put against the charter.** The £44 4s remainder is only reachable against the **two-hand** figure of £210, exactly as the review's disposition sets it. **Do not recompute the remainder with one hand.**
- The district's own fund is **two hundred and forty pounds a year on paper and nil in fact**, and the cartage that fed it has been nil since the fourteenth of the month before last. **The whole of the sitting at the First Verdict on the twenty-fifth of the fourth month, four months and twenty-two names, cost four pounds fifteen, of which two days were a clerk's own and out of her own purse, and it was entered against the ninth bridge's district road fund, which is not the district asked.** A place on a docket board costs about two days of a clerk's time and about four pounds of cart hire. **Four pounds fifteen is about a tenth of what is left of that fund.**
- **THE HUNDRED AND FIFTY HAULS BELONG TO WENNA TACE AND TO NOBODY ELSE. They are the ones she hauled herself in the last eleven months (Chapter 64) and they are the ones with no figure, because a haul in this district cannot be priced at all. Do not give them to the gate hand.**
- **The thirty years is now also six thousand hauls at two thirds**, in Bryn Sallow's own words at the gate, at her own asking, entered on the sixteenth of the fourth month and read out twice at the called reading. She got the figure by watching the barrow go past her gate about two hundred times a year for thirty years, and there is no arithmetic in the county that turns ten and a half hours into a barrow. Her two figures are **one thousand four hundred and seventeen pounds** as entered and **one thousand two hundred and fifteen pounds** after her own correction, and **six thousand hauls at two thirds**, and none of the three makes another and **none can be paid.**

### The haul rate, and what this batch did and did not do to it

- **THE ENTRY IS CANON AND WAS NOT RE-DERIVED.** It says the district's own rate is a third of a haul rate, that a haul is paid at three times the district's own rate, and that a haul in this district is two thirds short of a haul. **This batch does not resolve that, does not re-state it as a self-cancelling sum, and does not put a second contradictory sentence into the instrument.** What it adds is the reason the sum cannot be priced at all: **the district's own rate is a rate for a *load*, and this district has no carts and has not had one in the living memory of anybody in it, so a barrow here is four loads to a cart that does not come, and the four in that sentence is a fact about a cart and not a price.** The hundred and fifty hauls therefore have no figure and will keep having none until the district agrees a rate for a load or enters that it never will. **Oakes is right that this is a hole and not a mistake and that a hole is worse and lasts longer.**
- **The water rate's arithmetic, which closes and is canon: half a farthing a bucket is an eighth of a penny; eight buckets a day is a penny; a penny a day is seven pence a week, which is one pound ten shillings and fourpence a year; in a district of about two hundred and forty people that rate falls on Ottoline Fenn at eight times what it falls on most of the rest of them.** Do not use one pound eight shillings and fourpence. Do not use forty times.

### The people this batch made

- **Wist Anhalt, forty-nine, keeper of the fourth bridge for twenty-nine years.** The deck has had four square holes in it since the twentieth of the first month and **has been open to the weather since the twenty-seventh of the third month**, six days on the second of the fourth. **The four new timbers were cut on the road above the fen on the thirtieth of the third month and are lying in the mud on the north bank because there is no water in the river to float a timber up to a deck and the man who would do it has not come.** **Nobody asked him and nobody has given him a second want. He does not know that Ilyan cannot read and never will from this case.**
- **Garvin Slade, thirty-eight**, sells salt out of a booth at the top of Weir Lane **since he was thirteen, which is twenty-five years**, and lives in a house he owns on Weir Lane. **He is the man Bryn Sallow has bought salt off since she was nineteen.** He became one of the four of the Halloway bench on the twenty-eighth of the third month without knowing what a bench was. **On the sixteenth of the fourth month he withdrew his consent in writing, in his own hand, in about two dictated sentences, and his name is now in the district's book twice on purpose.** A rate cannot be withdrawn because it was read aloud on the first of the month to about two hundred people, and a clerk does not rub out a name. **A withdrawal is a different kind of object from a rate: nobody can be inside one; a person can be next to one.** His cost: he cannot un-say it, about two hundred and forty people on his lane will know inside a month, and he is the reason a woman of about thirty with a job at the second bell has to decide whether to stand in a crowd.
- **Ottoline Fenn, sixty-one**, takes in washing at the upper end of the street and there are four women who wash with her. She has drawn at the low-water standpipe for nineteen years with a yoke and two buckets, four times a day. **Since the sixth of the fourth month she fills at the ninth bridge's pump, four miles up the fen road, at the fourth bell before the count, and walks back. She has told exactly one person — Slade, at his counter, on the eighteenth — and has not told the district, which still believes she comes down to the pump every morning. Nobody collects buckets, so the lost penny cannot be entered as a loss.** She is the only person in this batch who is not in the case and is not a party to anything.
- **Merrick Vaunt, nineteen**, a lighterman's mate on the low water, taught letters at nine and can get most of a sentence but not the whole. He is the boy who asked what a haul was at the first quarter's reading. **He is not a holder, not a clerk, not paid, and was not asked; Wenna Tace asked him on the fifth of the fourth month and pays for the paper out of a pound and eleven a quarter.** He reads the book twice a week in a room over a pump and he is the reader in the rule of the twenty-ninth. He is not the man of nineteen on the docket board, who is a different person.
- **Aldis Wray, forty-six**, a landlord with four rooms, not a bad man, put Fallowditch's own answer face up on a table in the middle of March, read it out when he did not have to, and came back on the second of the fourth month with nothing.
- **Harl Mecken, sixty**, a deck foreman at the cut banks who has had nine apprentices and has buried two of them in trades they did not finish. T. Blore came down to him on the second of the second month and works there. **He is not Ordwin Sarn and the cut banks still has one keeper.**
- **Maren Kett, nineteen years in the Orison chamber**, returned on the twenty-fifth of the fourth month. **He argued against his own client for three hours** (not an hour — Chapter 68's scene says three). **HIS PRESENCE IS: the twenty-fifth of the fourth month in Ch 89; the twentieth of the fifth month in Ch 96, where he enters a question in his own book on the eighteenth; and the twenty-third and twenty-fourth of the sixth in Ch 99. (Corrected by the Volume 02 audit: an earlier version of this line said he had not been in the case since the seventeenth of the second month, which is contradicted by Chapter 96, and Chapter 99 line 77's "has not been in this case since the twenty-fifth of the fourth month" is contradicted by Chapter 96 and is a defect in that chapter.)** He is a **counsel**, not a clerk. **What he pays for asking is not a name on a board: a counsel who enters a question in the yard is not a witness to it, cannot be examined on it, cannot be served with it, and cannot carry it to the people who wrote the words, because there is no party in the matter and a question of his does not travel without one.** The board went to twenty-one on the fourteenth of the third month when Abel Kearn withdrew and back to twenty-two on the twenty-fifth of the fourth — forty-two days — and it has been twenty-two for nineteen years. **Chapter 89 references Kearn's withdrawal once in a single clause and does not perform it.**
- **Dessin Nahl, clerk of the Orison chamber**, wrote the second twenty-second name in her own hand on the twenty-fifth of the fourth and is the one clerk in this case who has read the whole of it.

### The two rules, and the reading at the pump

- **Fallowditch's rule of the ninth of the fourth month, made in its own hand, in its own room, in public, on a day it appointed, by a bench of three: any person of the district may bring a document and it shall be read out, and an entry made with a date and the name of the person it is about; the document goes back to the person and the entry stays; a person may ask for an entry without a name, and the entry then says UNNAMED, and the bench enters that an UNNAMED entry in a district this size is one everybody can identify and that this is theatre and that the bench is doing it anyway; the day is paid out of the three of them out of their own purses at no rate; and nobody outside the district may use the room, because the bench is eleven miles from the fen road and four miles from its own farthest place and a room with a rule in it reaches the people who can get to it.** **It cost the bench eleven days of a boundary in three weeks and the boundary is not going to be done this spring.**
- **Halloway's rule of the seventeenth of the fourth month, made in about twenty minutes: where a person who has set a rate withdraws consent to it in writing after the rate has been read aloud, the rate shall be read aloud again inside the quarter, at the sixth bell, at the standpipe, by the holder, to whatever people come, and the withdrawal shall be read with it; the cost is about two hundred and forty people going up a bank in the cold, paid by the people who go up it, and the bench has no money and knows it; and this is not a right, a right is towards a person, this is towards a barrel lid.**
- **Halloway's rule of the twenty-ninth of the fourth month, drafted by the holder in her own page and copied by the clerk into the bench's book: the book is open at the pump two days a week at the fourth bell, any person of the district may ask for it to be read aloud to them, and a person so asking is not to be asked why. The reader is a boy of nineteen who is not a holder, is not a clerk, is not paid, and was not asked, and the bench enters that it is unpaid so that a clerk in four years does not read a generous district.** **It copies nothing and has read nothing of Fallowditch's book.** What it buys is that a person who cannot read the book can have it read to her, which is a rate that has not gone up, a page a person can use, and not a right that survives a district being squeezed.
- **The called reading was held on the nineteenth of the fourth month at the sixth bell, at the turn, to about two hundred and forty people in the cold.** The rate has not gone up and has not gone down, and it has been the same half a farthing a bucket since the seventeenth of the second month. The thirty years was read as **six thousand hauls at two thirds**, twice, and the hauler's own hundred and fifty was read as having no figure at all, and the two are different debts by different women in the same unwritable unit. **Bryn Sallow, two hundred feet away at the gate, heard it and stopped working the gate for four seconds, which she does not do.**

### The second ask

- **The carrier's clause has been used exactly twice in the fiction**, the first on the thirtieth of the second month for the district of Nethergill and the second on the thirteenth of the fourth month for the bench of three of Fallowditch. **Past the fourth mile it is a favour and cannot be entered, and Fallowditch is more than four miles away, and he walked and is paid nothing and the leg stopped two hundred yards short of the shop.** **The cost of the ask fell on the district asked: A DAY AND A HEDGE, and the day is Fallowditch's and the hedge is Fallowditch's (Chapter 85, line 109), out of nine pounds in the year, a day out of three people's pockets, and a third member who said *I would like to send it anyway* and asked for it to be entered that he said it out loud. (Corrected by the Volume 02 audit: an earlier version of this line gave four shillings and sixpence of cart, which no chapter gives for this ask; four shillings and sixpence is the cost of a different notice, in Chapter 96.)**
- **The licence's third term is the mechanism and Sera Quill's position is exact: the ask is valid on its face and is the second use of the third term; she may not send him, because a recordwright's carrier is a thing she is and not a thing she hands to the man the fourth term names; and if he goes he goes because a clause with his name in it says he goes, and those are two different sentences and she will not let a ledger read the second as the first.**
- **Perditta Nye has made herself a holder of a room nobody made her a holder of, in a district of about two hundred and forty people, and is the only person in it who can read, and her bench's ordinary work has stopped.** She wanted a copy of the terms of the district of Halloway, **eleven miles down the fen road**, because a room with a rule in it needs to know what a page is for.

### The continuity line

- **The eighth appearance is the line being read out in a room over a shop that had been a chandler's in Fallowditch on the thirteenth of the fourth month, at the foot of the ninth sheet, under the fifth term, in the copy that was carried and not in the sheet, by a clerk reading a sheet out word for word who did not know what it was.** The letter Nye wrote that afternoon went down to the fen road on the fourteenth, sat on a tray in a chamber with nothing on it asking anything, and was stamped on the **twenty-seventh** of the fourth month, when a clerk copying a list of a board picked it up because it was face up. It is a fortnight's journey and the number on the spine is the only thing that happened to it. **There is one document and it has one date. Do not give it a second.**
- **The ninth appearance is the sixth refusal**, in the same four words, at the counter in a stone room off the second gallery, with nobody named, and Harrow saying he will refuse on the seventh. **A seventh refusal is reserved for Chapters 91–100 and is a volume-level promise.**
- **The holder has still not read the line under the fifth term of the copy in her own room, and she reads a charter in order, and a charter ends at the fifth term. Nobody in this case will tell her, and nothing in this batch told her.**

### Ilyan: the seventh thing, and what it cost

- **THE SEVENTH THING, entered by him in his own hand on the twenty-sixth of the fourth month, at the second bell, in a room over a pump, with a clerk writing it down.** He was **produced as a witness a second time** in a district he does not live in, about a number he put in a bench's book without the consent of the person it was owed to, who refused it, who was not compelled, and who cannot be overrule. **A witness can be produced twice and can be produced three times. He was asked three questions in advance by a clerk who has read four sheets of his own figures aloud in her own office by herself, and he was told the three questions in advance, and that is not what a witness usually gets.**
- **AND THE PART THAT COSTS: from the twenty-sixth of the fourth month he is not counting the gate hand's tide. The hours were wrong, the rate is wrong and cannot be corrected, and the figure cannot be struck out of a book that is not his, so the number stands and is read aloud four times a year and is wrong. And the only man in this city who could have made it more right by counting more carefully has stopped, and nobody asked him to.** The reason he gives is in the body: counting is a burden of his own and the only thing that ever cleared his leg, and he has worked out that he has been holding a woman up for three hours a day so that a bench of four can read a number, and the number is wrong, and the woman said no. He enters that he stopped for a reason that is about his own body and not about her, that he believes that, and that he would like it entered that he believes it.
- **The leg is his own now and does not clear.** The low water is in two layers with a third under both, the door is at the same height and the same distance, and the rendering is unchanged in kind and unresolved: a room, boards, a table the size of a table a hand could go on, a lamp with the wick down, a window with weather on the wrong side of it, four people at the table, one of them about forty-four with her back to him and her right foot up on something. **The token is not touched. The vantage has not moved. The Shale Mirror question is not touched.**
- **Bryn Sallow's answer, at the gate, at the turn, and it is the best sentence in the batch: you have made yourself a man who cannot be produced on a number; and the other thing, which he did not ask for, is that she has been counting her own tide in her own feet since she was fourteen, that there is no slate, that she would have gone on with it, and that in about four years a clerk will read a book in which a man says he stopped for a woman and there will be nothing at all in it about the woman. She still does not know what is in his leg.**
- **Corva Silt is still the second person in the world who knows he cannot read, and it is not on a page.** She is now the person who produced him, who read four sheets of his own figures aloud in her own office by herself, who put the three questions to him in advance, and who said out loud once, off the record, that a number can be wrong in a way that can be argued about and that it is the only door anybody has ever found out of a shape and that it is not a good door.

### Panel accounting for Batch 0004

- **TWO System-rendered entries, one each in Chapters 83 and 86**, at most one in any chapter, and none stating a general rule: the question and the answer in a room over a pump, and the three sums on a slate. **A third panel, in Chapter 88, was cut in the second review pass** because it restated a document already rendered in the same chapter and put Ilyan at a pump the chapter never puts him at. **Both surviving panels are plain bold lines with no blockquote marker, each introduced by a narrative line.** The batch stayed well under its allowance of five. **Every block-quoted block in Chapters 81–90 is a physical object in the fiction.** **There is no bold used as emphasis in running prose or dialogue anywhere in Chapters 81–90**, and a review pass stripped the three instances that were there.
- **Word range for the batch: 3,093–5,110.** Chapter 90 carries the production, the seventh thing, the sixth refusal, the rule of the twenty-ninth and the batch close, and is not padded. Chapter 88 is the shortest and is a complete scene. **Do not treat any of these figures as a target.**
- **A review agent returned forty-two hard findings and eighteen soft ones against this batch and all are fixed; they are listed in `state/batch-summary.md`. That is a review, not a closure claim.**
